## PREFACE

For anyone who wishes to look into the inner soul of the East Asian <note>original: "Oriental"</note> people and see the unique spiritual beings among whom they live, the following stories will serve as true guides. They are rooted in the three great religions of East Asia: Taoism, Buddhism, and Confucianism.

An old manuscript copy of Im Bang’s stories came into the translator's hands a year ago. He now presents them to the Western world so that they may serve as an introduction to the mysteries—and what many call the absurdities—of Asia. Some of these stories are indeed very gruesome and unpleasant, but they faithfully portray the conditions under which Im Bang himself, and many past generations of Koreans, lived.

The thirteen short stories by Yi Ryuk are taken from a reprint of old Korean writings published in 1911 by a Japanese company. Three anonymous stories are also added: “The Geomancer” <term>geomancer: a person who practices divination by interpreting markings on the ground or the local landscape, often to find lucky sites for graves or homes</term>, to show how Mother Earth has caused anxiety for her human children; and “Im, the

<summary>This preface introduces a collection of Korean folklore and ghost stories, explaining that they represent the spiritual lives and religious traditions (Taoism, Buddhism, and Confucianism) of the Korean people.</summary>
<keywords>Im Bang, Yi Ryuk, Korea, folklore, Taoism, Buddhism, Confucianism, geomancy, 1911</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface, identifying specific stories in the collection and the historical sources used for biographical information.</meta>

<page-num>viii</page-num>
<header>PREFACE</header>

Hunter," to tell of the realities that exist in the spiritual realm <note>original: "upper air"</note>; and "The Man who lost his Legs," as an example of a Korean Sinbad <note>Sinbad: the legendary sailor from "One Thousand and One Nights," known for his fantastic travels and survival</note>.

The biographical notes that accompany the stories are taken very largely from the *Kuk-cho In-mul-chi* <note>Kuk-cho In-mul-chi: "Korea’s Record of Famous Men," a classic biographical dictionary of notable Korean figures</note>.

<meta>This page provides biographical sketches of the two primary authors or compilers of the stories in this collection: Im Bang and Yi Ryuk. It follows the table of contents and sets the historical context for the tales.</meta>

<page-num>xi</page-num>

# BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCHES

**IM BANG** was born in 1640, the son of a provincial governor. He was very bright as a boy and, from his earliest years, was fond of study, eventually becoming a great scholar. He passed his first civil service examinations <note>original: "matriculated"</note> at the top of his class in 1660 and graduated in 1663. He was a follower <note>original: "disciple"</note> of Song Si-yol, one of Korea’s most prominent writers and neo-Confucian scholars. In 1719, during his eightieth year, he became the governor of Seoul and also held the office of Secretary of the Cabinet. In 1721, he fell into political trouble over the choice of the Heir Apparent <note>The Heir Apparent is the designated successor to the throne; this refers to the intense factional struggle regarding who would succeed King Gyeongjong.</note>. In 1722, because of his role in a government disturbance, he was exiled to North Korea <note>During the Joseon dynasty, political exiles were often sent to the remote and rugged northern border provinces as punishment.</note>, where he died.

(From *Kuk-cho In-mul-chi* <note>original: "Gukjo Inmulji"</note>, “Korea’s Record of Famous Men.”)

**YI RYUK** lived during the reign of King Se-jo <note>King Se-jo reigned from 1455 to 1468.</note>. He passed his first examinations <note>original: "matriculated"</note> in 1459 and graduated first in his class in 1564. <note>The date 1564 appears to be a typographical error in the original text, as the subject began his studies in 1459. It is more likely to have been 1464, during the reign of King Sejo.</note> He was a man who held many government offices and received many distinctions for his literary excellence.

->“Korea’s Record of Famous Men.”<-

<summary>This page provides brief biographies of Im Bang (1640–1724) and Yi Ryuk (15th century), the Korean scholars whose writings and collections form the basis of this book.</summary>
<keywords>Im Bang, Song Si-yol, Yi Ryuk, King Se-jo, Gukjo Inmulji, Korean history, Joseon Dynasty, civil service examinations, political exile</keywords>

# KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS, AND FAIRIES

## I

### CHARAN

[Some think that love—strong, true, and self-sacrificing—is not to be found in the East <note>original: "Orient"</note>; but the story of Charan, which has been passed down for over four hundred years, proves the contrary. It still has the fresh, sweet flavor of a modern romance, even though the Eastern setting provides a unique and interesting background.]

During the reign of King Seongjong <note>original: "Sung-jong"</note> (1488–1495 AD), a prominent Korean man was appointed governor of P'yŏngan <note>original: "Pyong-an"</note> Province. P'yŏngan is considered the foremost of the eight provinces in terms of achievements in learning <note>original: "erudition"</note> and high society. Many of its <term>literati</term> <note>Scholars or intellectuals who were well-versed in literature, music, and the arts.</note> are skilled musicians and demonstrate great talent in the affairs of state.

<vocab>King Seongjong, Charan, P'yŏngan Province, literati, Korea</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the story of Charan, a historical romance from 15th-century Korea, and describes P'yŏngan Province as a center of culture and scholarship.</summary>
<keywords>King Seongjong, Charan, P'yŏngan Province, Joseon Dynasty, Korean folklore, literati</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Charan, introduced in the previous page as a famous historical romance from the reign of King Seongjong.</meta>

# KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES

At the time of this story, there was a famous dancing-girl in P'yŏngan <note>original: "Pyong-an"</note> named Charan. She was very beautiful, and she sang and danced to the delight of everyone who saw her. Her abilities were also quite remarkable, for she understood the classics and was well-acquainted with history. She was the most brilliant of all the <term>gisaeng</term> <note>original: "geisha"; professional female entertainers in Korea, similar to the Japanese geisha, who were highly trained in the arts and conversation.</note>, and she was famous and widely renowned.

The Governor's family included a sixteen-year-old son whose face was as handsome as a painting. Although he was very young, he was thoroughly grounded in the Chinese classics and was a gifted scholar. His judgment was excellent, and he had a fine appreciation for literary style. The moment he lifted his pen, his writing took on a beautiful and impressive form. He became known as Keydong, which means "The Gifted Lad." The Governor had no other children—neither son nor daughter—so his heart was entirely devoted to this boy. 

On the boy's birthday, the Governor invited all the local officials and other special guests to drink to his health. A company of dancing-girls and a large band of musicians were also present. During a lull in the banquet, the Governor called for his son. He ordered the leader of the dancing-girls to choose the prettiest among them so that she and his son could dance together to entertain the assembled guests. Upon hearing this, the company unanimously called for Charan.

<summary>This page introduces the two protagonists: Charan, a brilliant and beautiful gisaeng, and Keydong, the Governor's talented only son. During a birthday banquet, the Governor requests that the two dance together to entertain his guests.</summary>

<keywords>Charan, P'yŏngan, gisaeng, Keydong, The Gifted Lad, Governor's son, Korean folklore, Joseon scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Charan and the Governor's son, describing their deepening bond and the parents' dilemma as their time in the northern province ends.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>CHARAN</header>

...as the one suited by her talents, achievements, and age to be a fitting partner for his son. They came out and danced like fairies, as graceful as swaying willow branches and as light and airy as swallows. Everyone who saw them was charmed. The Governor was also greatly pleased. He called Charan to him, invited her to sit on the dais <note>original: "dais"; a raised platform for guests of honor.</note>, shared the banquet with her, and gave her a gift of silk. He then commanded that from that day forward, she would be the personal entertainer <note>original: "dancing maiden"</note> assigned to his son.

From that birthday on, they became the closest of friends. They cared for each other deeply. Their love was more remarkable than all the delightful stories in history—a love such as had never been seen before.

The Governor’s term of office was extended for six more years, and so they remained in the northern province <note>original: "north country"; referring to P'yŏngan, where the story is set.</note>. Finally, when the time came for them to return home to the capital, the Governor and his wife were very anxious about their son being separated from Charan. If they forced them to separate, they feared their son would die of a broken heart. If they took her with them, since she was not his wife, they feared it would damage his reputation. They could not reach a decision, so they decided to leave the matter to their son himself. 

They called him and said, “Even parents cannot decide how their son should feel about a young woman. What should we do? You love Charan so much that it will be very hard for you to part, and yet to have an entertainer <note>original: "dancing-girl"</note> before...

<summary>Charan and the Governor's son become inseparable during their six years in the north. As the family prepares to return to the capital, the parents struggle with whether to bring Charan along or separate the lovers.</summary>
<keywords>Charan, Governor's son, P'yŏngan, romance, social class, gisaeng, parental dilemma</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Charan and the Governor's son, Keydong. Having been inseparable for six years in the northern province, they must now face the social consequences of their relationship as the family prepares to return to the capital, Seoul.</meta>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

...before you are married is considered bad etiquette and will interfere with your marriage prospects and promotion. However, having a second wife <note>original: "second wife"; referring to the Korean custom of taking a concubine or secondary wife from a lower social class.</note> is a common custom in Korea, and one that society recognizes. Do as you think best in the matter." The son replied, "There is no difficulty. When she is before my eyes, of course, she is everything; but when the time comes for me to start for home, she will be like a pair of worn-out shoes, set aside. So please, do not be anxious."

The Governor and his wife were greatly delighted and said he was a "superior man" <note>original: "superior man"; a term for a "Junzi," the Confucian ideal of a person who possesses high moral character and emotional restraint.</note> indeed.

When the time came to part, Charan wept bitterly, so much so that those standing by could hardly bear to look at her; but the son showed not the slightest sign of emotion. Those looking on were filled with wonder at his fortitude. Although he had already loved Charan for six years, he had never been separated from her for a single day, so he did not know what it meant to say goodbye, nor did he know how it felt to be parted.

The Governor returned to Seoul to fill the office of Chief Justice, and the son came also. After this return, thoughts of love for Charan consumed Keydong, though he never expressed them in word or manner. It was almost the time for the *Kam-see* Examination <note>original: "Kam-see Examination"; the preliminary civil service examination held in the provinces or the capital.</note>. The father, therefore, ordered his son to go with some of his friends to a neighbor—

<summary>To reassure his parents, Keydong pretends he will easily discard Charan like "worn shoes," but after returning to Seoul, he finds himself secretly consumed by grief and longing for her.</summary>
<keywords>Keydong, Charan, Seoul, Governor, Chief Justice, Kam-see Examination, Confucian ideals, social status</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Keydong, the Chief Justice's son. Distraught by his separation from Charan, he has been sent to a monastery to focus on his studies for the civil service examinations, but his longing for her has reached a breaking point.</meta>

<page-num>5</page-num>
<header>CHARAN</header>

...neighboring monastery to study and prepare. They went, and one night, after the day's work was over and everyone was asleep, the young man stole out into the courtyard. It was winter, with frost and snow and a cold, clear moon. The mountains were deep and the world was quiet, so that the slightest sound could be heard. The young man looked up at the moon and his thoughts were full of sorrow. He so wished to see Charan that he could no longer control himself; fearing that he would lose his reason, he decided that very night to set out for far-distant Pyong-an. 

He was wearing a fur head-dress, a thick coat, a leather belt, and a heavy pair of shoes. When he had gone less than ten *lee* <term>lee: a traditional Korean unit of distance, approximately 393 meters or about one-quarter of a mile</term>, however, his feet were blistered. He had to go into a neighboring village and change his leather shoes for straw sandals, and his expensive head-cover for an ordinary servant’s hat. 

He went on his way, begging as he went. He was often very hungry, and when night came, he was very, very cold. He was a rich man's son and had always dressed in silk and eaten dainty fare <note>original: "dainty fare"; refers to refined or high-quality food suitable for the upper class</note>, and had never in his life walked more than a few feet from his father's door. Now there lay before him a journey of hundreds of miles. He went stumbling along through the snow, making but poor progress. Hungry and frozen nearly to death, he had never known such suffering before. His clothes were

<summary>Driven by his love for Charan, Keydong abandons his studies and his life of privilege to travel hundreds of miles on foot to Pyong-an. He quickly suffers from the harsh winter and the physical toll of a journey for which his wealthy upbringing never prepared him.</summary>
<keywords>Keydong, Charan, Pyong-an, journey, social class, suffering, devotion, Korea</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Keydong, the son of the former Chief Justice. Having fled his studies and a life of luxury, he has endured a grueling winter journey on foot to find his beloved Charan.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>

### KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES

His clothes were torn, and his face became worn down and blackened until he looked like a goblin. Still, he pressed on, little by little, day after day, until at last, when a whole month had passed, he reached Pyong-an.

He went straight to Charan’s home, but Charan was not there—only her mother was. She looked at him but did not recognize him. He told her that he was the former Governor’s son and that out of love for Charan, he had walked five hundred <term>lee</term> <note>lee: a traditional Korean unit of distance, approximately 393 meters or about one-quarter of a mile; 500 lee is roughly 125 miles</note>. “Where is she?” he asked. When the mother heard this, instead of being pleased, she was very angry. She said, “My daughter is now with the son of the new Governor, and I never see her at all; she never comes home, and she has been away for two or three months. Even though you have made this long journey, there is no possible way for you to meet her.”

Her welcome was so cold that she did not even invite him inside. He thought to himself, “I came to see Charan, but she is not here. Her mother rejects me; I cannot go back, and I cannot stay. What shall I do?” While he was in this dilemma, a plan occurred to him. There was a scribe in Pyong-an who, during his father’s term of office, had committed an offense and been sentenced to death. However, there were mitigating circumstances <note>original: "extenuating circumstances"</note>. When the young man used to pay his morning respects <note>original: "salutations"</note> to his father, he had pleaded for and secured the man's pardon. Out of regard for his son’s petition, his father had forgiven the—

Keydong asked about the plan. <header>II</header>

## THE STORY OF CHANG TO-RYONG

[Taoism has been one of the great religions of Korea. Its main philosophy is expressed in the phrase *su-sim yon-song*, which means “to correct the mind and reform the nature.” In contrast, Buddhism’s core philosophy is *myong-sim kyon-song*, meaning “to enlighten the heart and see the soul.”

The goal of all Taoists is “eternal life” (*chang-saing pul-sa*); that of the Buddhists is to rid oneself of physical existence. In the Taoist world of the immortals <note>original: "genii"</note>, there are three great divisions: the upper immortals, who live with God; the middle immortals, who deal with the world of angels and spirits; and the lower immortals, who rule in sacred places on the earth among the hills, just as we find in the story of Chang To-ryong.]

During the reign of King Jungjong <note>original: "Chung-jong"</note> (r. 1506–1544) <note>King Jungjong was the eleventh king of the Joseon Dynasty, known for his efforts at government reform.</note>, there lived a beggar in Seoul whose face was extremely ugly and always dirty. He was about forty years old, but he still wore his hair in a long braid down his back like an unmarried boy <note>In traditional Korean society, a man only put his hair up into a topknot upon marriage; an older man with a braid was a sign of low social status or a lack of means to marry.</note>. He carried

<summary>This page introduces the story of Chang To-ryong by explaining the distinctions between Taoist and Buddhist goals in Korea and describing a mysterious, middle-aged beggar living in Seoul.</summary>

<keywords>Chang To-ryong, Taoism, Buddhism, King Jungjong, Seoul, immortals, Korean folklore, Joseon Dynasty</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction of the beggar Chang To-ryong, picking up mid-sentence from the previous page's description of his appearance and habits.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>THE STORY OF CHANG TO-RYONG</header>

...a bag over his shoulder and went about the streets begging. During the day, he went from one part of the city to the other, visiting each section, and when night came on, he would huddle up beside someone’s gate and go to sleep. He was frequently seen in <term>Chong-no</term> <note>original: "Bell Street"; this was the main commercial thoroughfare of historic Seoul</note> in the company of the servants and underlings of the rich. He and they were great friends, joking and bantering whenever they met. He used to say that his name was Chang, and so they called him <term>Chang To-ryong</term> <note>To-ryong (modern: doryeong) refers to an unmarried boy or the son of a gentleman; using this title for a middle-aged beggar was a form of lighthearted irony</note>. 

At that time, the magician <term>Chon U-chi</term> <note>A famous legendary figure in Korean folklore, known as a powerful Taoist wizard and trickster</note>, who was famous for his pride and arrogance, would dismount from his horse and prostrate himself most humbly whenever he met Chang while passing along the street. Not only did he bow, but he seemed to regard Chang with the greatest fear, so much so that he did not dare look him in the face. Chang, sometimes without even nodding his head, would say, “Well, how goes it with you, eh?” Chon, with his hands respectfully tucked in his sleeves, would reply, “Very well, sir, thank you, very well.” Fear was written across all his features whenever he faced Chang.

Sometimes, too, when Chon would bow, Chang would refuse to notice him at all and go by without a word. Those who saw it were astonished and asked Chon the reason. Chon said in reply,

<summary>This page describes the beggar Chang To-ryong's life in the streets of Seoul and reveals a mysterious dynamic where the powerful magician Chon U-chi treats the humble beggar with extreme fear and reverence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the story of Chang To-ryong, specifically Chon U-chi’s explanation of Chang's true nature and Chang's subsequent interaction with a local official.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
# KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES

> "There are only three <term>spirit-men</term> <note>original: "spirit-men"; likely referring to *sinseon*, Taoist immortals or enlightened beings with supernatural powers</note> at present in <term>Choseon</term> <note>original: "Cho-sen"; the name of the Korean kingdom during this period</note>, of whom the greatest is Chang To-ryong; the second is Cheung Puk-chang; and the third is Yun Se-pyong. People of the world do not know it, but I do. Such being the case, should I not bow before him and show him reverence?"

Those who heard this explanation, knowing that Chon himself was a strange being, paid no attention to it.

At that time in Seoul, there was a certain <term>literary undergraduate</term> <note>original: "literary undergraduate"; a person who had passed the lower-level civil service examinations (likely a *saengwon* or *jinsa*) and held a minor office</note> in office whose house was right on the street. This man used to see Chang frequently going about begging, and one day he called him and asked who he was, and why he begged. Chang answered, "I was originally from a cultured family in <term>Jeolla Province</term> <note>original: "Chulla Province"; a region in southwestern Korea</note>, but my parents died of typhus fever, and I had no brothers or relatives left to share my fate. I alone remained of all my clan, and having no home of my own, I have gone about begging and have at last reached Seoul. As I am not skilled in any trade and do not know <term>Chinese characters</term> <note>original: "Chinese letters"; referring to Hanja, the classical Chinese script used for formal writing and government business in Korea</note>, what else can I do?" The undergraduate, hearing that he was a scholar, felt very sorry for him, gave him food and drink, and refreshed him.

From this time on, whenever there was any special celebration at his home, he used to call Chang in and have him share it.

# THE STORY OF CHANG TO-RYONG

On a certain day when the master was on his way to his office, he saw a dead body being carried on a stretcher toward the <term>Water Gate</term> <note>original: "Water Gate"; likely *Gwanghuimun*, one of the gates of Seoul through which corpses were traditionally carried out of the city</note>. Looking at it closely from the horse he was riding, he recognized it as the corpse of Chang To-ryong. He felt so sad that he turned back to his house and wept over it, saying, “There are many miserable people on earth, but who has ever seen one as miserable as poor Chang? As I count the time back on my fingers, he has been begging on <term>Bell Street</term> <note>original: "Bell Street"; referring to *Jongno*, the main street of Seoul</note> for fifteen years, and now he passes out of the city as a dead body.”

Twenty years and more afterward, the master had to make a journey through <term>South Jeolla Province</term> <note>original: "South Chulla Province"</note>. As he was passing <term>Jirisan Mountain</term> <note>original: "Chi-i Mountain"; one of the three most sacred mountains in Korea, often associated in folklore with Taoist immortals</note>, he lost his way and became lost in a maze among the hills. The day began to fade, and he could neither return nor go forward. He saw a narrow footpath, such as woodmen take, and turned into it to see if it led to any settlement. As he went along, there were rocks and deep ravines. Little by little, as he advanced farther, the scene changed and seemed to become strangely transformed. The farther he went, the more wonderful it became. After he had gone some miles, he discovered himself to be in another world entirely—no longer a world of earth and dust. He saw someone coming toward him dressed in ethereal green, mounted on a horse and carrying a

He never changed his clothes <note>original: "garb"</note>, and yet their appearance never varied in all those fifteen years. This alone would have marked him as a strange being, but our human <note>original: "fleshly"</note> eyes did not recognize it."

### A STORY OF THE FOX

[**THE FOX.**—People of the East say that among the long-lived creatures are the tortoise, the deer, the crane, and the fox, and that these long-lived animals attain special states of spiritual refinement. If trees exist through long ages, they become coal; if pine resin endures, it becomes amber. Similarly, if a fox lives for a long time—while it never becomes an angel or a spiritual being in the way a human does—it undergoes various transformations <note>original: "metamorphoses"</note> and appears on earth in various forms.]

Yi Kwai was the son of a government minister. He passed his civil service examinations and held high office. When his father served as the Governor of Pyong-an Province, Kwai was a young boy and accompanied him there. Since the Governor’s first wife had died, Kwai’s stepmother was the mistress of the home. Once, when His Excellency had gone out on an inspection tour, the official residence <term>yamen: the administrative office or residence of a government official</term> was left empty of other officials, and Kwai stayed there with her. In the rear garden of the official quarters was a pavilion called the Hill Pagoda, which was connected by a narrow gateway with the

### IV

## CHEUNG PUK-CHANG, THE SEER

[**CHEUNG PUK-CHANG.**—The *Yol-ryok Keui-sul* <note>original: *Yol-ryok Keui-sul*; an encyclopedic history of the Joseon Dynasty compiled by Yi Keung-ik</note>, one of Korea’s noted histories, says of Cheung Puk-chang that he was pure in purpose and without selfish ambition. He was superior to all others in his marvelous gifts. For him to read a book once was to know it by heart. There was nothing that he could not understand—astronomy, geology, music, medicine, mathematics, fortune-telling, and Chinese characters, which he knew by intuition and not from study.

He followed his father in the retinue <note>original: "in the train of"</note> of the envoy to Peking <note>modern-day Beijing</note>, and there talked to all the diverse peoples whom he met without any preparation. They all wondered at him and called him “The Mystery.” He knew, too, the meaning of the calls of birds and beasts; and while he lived in the mountains, he could see and tell what people were doing in the distant valley, describing what was going on in each house, which, upon investigation, was found in each case to be true. He was a Taoist <note>A follower of Taoism, which in Korea often blended philosophy with esoteric practices and the pursuit of spiritual immortality</note>, and received strange revelations.

<vocab>Cheung Puk-chang, Yol-ryok Keui-sul, Korea, Peking, Taoist, seer</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces Cheung Puk-chang, a legendary Korean scholar and Taoist seer celebrated for his photographic memory, mastery of diverse sciences, and supernatural abilities such as understanding animals and clairvoyance.</summary>
<keywords>Cheung Puk-chang, Yol-ryok Keui-sul, Taoism, clairvoyance, Joseon Dynasty, polymath, divination</keywords>

While he was in Beijing <note>original: Peking</note>, he met envoys from the Court of Ryukyu <note>original: Loochoo; the Ryukyu Islands, modern-day Okinawa</note>, who were also prophets. While in their own country, they had studied the stars and horoscopes, and upon traveling to China, they knew they were destined to meet a Holy Man <note>A term for a sage or person of great spiritual attainment</note>. As they traveled, they asked about this mysterious being until they finally reached Beijing. Inquiring as they went, they moved from one diplomatic station to another until they finally met Cheung Puk-chang. At that moment, a great awe came over them, and they bowed down to the ground before him.

They took from their baggage a little book inscribed with these words:

> “In such a year, on such a day, at such an hour, in such a place, you shall meet a Holy Man.”

“If this does not refer to Your Excellency,” they said, “who else could it be?” They requested that he teach them the sacred *Book of Changes* <note>original: *I Ching*; an ancient Chinese manual of divination and philosophy</note>, and he responded by teaching it to them in their own native language. At that time, when the other envoys heard of this, they competed with each other to see who would be first to meet the marvelous stranger, and he spoke to every person in their own language. All of them were greatly amazed and said, “He is truly a man of God.”

Someone asked him, “There are those who understand the sounds of birds and animals, but foreign languages must be learned to be known; how can you speak them without study?”

Puk-chang replied, “I do not know them from study...”

# CHEUNG PUK-CHANG, THE SEER

"...without having learned them, but knowing them instinctively <note>original: "unconsciously"</note>."

Puk-chang was familiar with the three great religions, but he considered Confucianism to be the primary one. “Its traditional writings,” he said, “teach us filial piety and reverence. The learning of the Sages deals with human relationships and not with spiritual mysteries; however, Taoism and Buddhism deal with the examination of the soul and the heart, and so with heavenly matters <note>original: "things above"</note> rather than earthly matters. This is the difference.”

At thirty-two years of age, he passed his civil service exams <note>original: "matriculated"</note>, but he had no interest in further literary or academic study. Instead, he became a government teacher of medicine, astrology, and mathematics.

We are told he was a skilled whistler. Once, when he had climbed to the highest peak of the Diamond Mountains <note>Geumgangsan; a mountain range in modern-day North Korea famous for its scenic beauty and many Buddhist temples</note> and whistled, the echoes rang through the hills. The monks were startled and wondered who was playing such a beautiful flute.

There is a term in Korea, *hyean-bang-gwang* <note>original: *he-an pang-kwang*</note>, which means “spiritual-eye distant-vision”—the ability to see things from a great distance. This ability is attributed to both Taoists and Buddhists.

It is said that when a student reaches a certain stage in their spiritual progress, the soft part of the head <note>The fontanelle or crown of the head, often associated in Eastern meditation with the "Crown Chakra" or the point where spiritual energy exits the body</note> returns to the primal thinness seen in a...

<summary>This page describes Cheung Puk-chang’s philosophical views on the three religions and his preference for practical sciences over traditional literature. It also details his supernatural whistling and the concept of "spiritual-eye distant-vision."</summary>

<keywords>Cheung Puk-chang, Confucianism, Taoism, Buddhism, Diamond Mountains, spiritual-eye, hyean-bang-gwang, Geumgangsan, Korean folklore</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual vision (hyean-bang-gwang) and the physical signs of enlightenment mentioned on the previous page regarding the sage Cheung Puk-chang.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>

->KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES<-

...causing it to rise and fall when the child breathes. Five rays of light emerge from this part of the head, radiating outward and upward as the student progresses along the spiritual path. As these rays extend further, the student’s spiritual vision is perfected, until eventually a Korean practitioner who is sufficiently advanced could sit and say, “In London today, such and such a great event is taking place.”

For example, Seo Hwa-dam <note>original: "So Wha-tam"; a famous 16th-century Korean Neo-Confucian philosopher and Taoist scholar</note>, who was a Taoist Sage, was once seen laughing to himself while sitting with his eyes closed. When asked why he laughed, he replied, “At this very moment in the Ha-in Monastery <note>Haeinsa; one of the three largest Buddhist temples in Korea, located about 300 miles from the capital</note>, a great feast is being held. The priest stirring the massive kettle of bean gruel has fallen in, but the others are unaware and are eating the soup.” News arrived from the monastery later that proved what the sage had seen was actually true.

The history of Confucius also mentions this phenomenon when it tells of him climbing the Tai Mountains of Shandong <note>original: "Shan-tung"; a sacred mountain range in China</note> with his disciple Yan Hui <note>original: "An-ja"; the most esteemed of Confucius's disciples</note> and looking toward the Kingdom of Wu <note>original: "On"</note>. Confucius asked Yan Hui if he could see anything, and Yan Hui replied, “I see white horses tied at the gates of Wu.”

Confucius replied, “No, no, your vision is still imperfect; stop looking. Those are not white horses, but rolls of white silk hung out for bleaching.”

<sig>D</sig>

### *The Story*

The Master, Puk-chang, was a famous Korean. From the time of his birth, he was a miraculous mystery. When reading a book, if he only glanced through it, he could recall it word for word. Without any special study, he became a master of astronomy, geology, medicine, fortune-telling, music, mathematics, and geomancy <note>original: "geomancy"; the traditional practice of *pungsu*, or arranging buildings and graves in harmony with the natural energy of the earth</note>. He was such a true specialist that he mastered them all.

He was also thoroughly knowledgeable in the three great religions: Confucianism, Buddhism, and Taoism. He spoke constantly about things that other people could not possibly understand. He understood the sounds of birds, the voices of Nature, and many other things. During his childhood, he accompanied his father when he went as an envoy <note>a diplomatic representative</note> to Peking <note>modern-day Beijing</note>. At that time, various foreign peoples <note>original: "barbarian peoples"; a term used historically in East Asia to refer to ethnic groups outside the immediate cultural sphere of China and Korea</note> also came to pay their tribute. Puk-chang became acquainted with them on the journey. After hearing their languages only once, he was easily able to communicate with them. His own countrymen who accompanied him were not the only ones astonished; the Chinese people <note>original: "Chinamen"</note> and the foreigners themselves were equally amazed. There are many interesting stories hinted at in the history of Puk-chang, but few proper records were made of them, and so many have been lost.

<vocab>Cheung Puk-chang, Seer, Korean, astronomy, geology, medicine, fortune-telling, music, mathematics, geomancy, Confucianism, Buddhism, Taoism, Peking, envoy, tribute, Chinamen</vocab>

There is one story, however, that I recall which comes to me through trustworthy witnesses: Puk-chang, on a certain day, went to visit his paternal aunt. She asked him to be seated, and as they talked together, she said to him, “I had some harvesting to do in the Yongnam region <note>original: "Yong-nam County"; Yongnam is the traditional name for the Gyeongsang provinces in southeastern Korea</note>, and I sent a servant to see to it. His return is overdue, and yet he does not come. I am afraid he has encountered thieves, or chanced upon a fire or some other misfortune.”

Puk-chang replied, “Shall I tell you how it goes with him, and how far he has come on the way?”

She laughed, saying, “Do you mean to joke about it?”

Puk-chang, from where he was sitting, looked off apparently toward the far south, and at last said to his aunt, “He is just now crossing the hill called Bird Pass <note>original: "Bird Pass"; known in Korean as *Choryeong* or *Saejae*, this was a strategic and famous mountain pass on the main road to Seoul</note> in Mungyeong County, Gyeongsang Province. Look! He is getting a beating just now from a passing *yangban* <note>original: "yangban"; the traditional ruling class or gentry of dynastic Korea</note>, but I see it is his own fault, so you do not need to trouble yourself about him.”

The aunt laughed, and asked, “Why should he be beaten; what is the reason, pray?”

Puk-chang replied, “It seems this official was eating his dinner at the top of the hill when your servant rode by him without dismounting. The gentleman was naturally very angry and had his...”

The aunt could not believe it was true and treated the matter as a joke; yet Puk-chang did not seem to be joking at all.

Interested and curious, she made a note of the date on the wall after Puk-chang had departed. When the servant finally returned, she asked him what day he had crossed Bird Pass <note>original: "Bird Pass"; known in Korean as *Choryeong* or *Saejae*, this was a strategic and famous mountain pass on the main road to Seoul</note>, and it matched the date she had recorded. She also asked, "Did you get into trouble with a *yangban* <term>yangban: a member of the traditional ruling class or gentry in dynastic Korea</term> while passing through there?"

The servant gave her a startled look and asked, "How do you know?" He then told her everything that had happened to him, and it was exactly as Puk-chang had described it, even down to the smallest detail.

->IM BANG <note>Im Bang (1640–1724) was a prominent Korean scholar and official known for collecting tales of the supernatural.</note><-

[Yun Se-pyong was a man from Seoul who lived to be over ninety years old. A little later, servants brought in a small dining table of rice for each person in the group. Chon took a mouthful of his rice and then blew it out toward the courtyard. The rice immediately transformed into beautiful butterflies that flew gaily away.

Yun Se-pyong was a military man who rose to the rank of minister in the days of King Jungjong <note>original: "Choong-jong"; he reigned from 1506–1544</note>. It seems that Yun learned the doctrine of magic from a passing stranger whom he met on his way to Beijing <note>original: "Peking"</note> while traveling in the company of the royal envoy. When

At home, he lived in a separate house, quite apart from the other members of his family. He was a man so greatly feared that even his wife and children did not dare to approach him. No one seemed to know what he did in secret. In winter, he was seen placing iron cleats <note>original: "iron cleats"; likely a reference to heated metal objects used in magical practices to regulate body temperature or display power</note> under each arm and changing them frequently; when they were removed, they appeared to be red-hot.

At the same time, there was a magician in Korea named Chon U-chi <note>Chon U-chi is a famous figure in Korean folklore, often depicted as a trickster-hero and Taoist wizard</note>, who traveled around Seoul practicing his craft. He was so skillful that he could even take on the appearance of the master of a house and enter the women's quarters <term>women's quarters</term><note>The *anbang*, the inner rooms of a traditional Korean house reserved for women</note> freely. Because of this, he was greatly feared and hated. Yun heard of him on several occasions and determined to rid the earth of him. Chon also heard of Yun and avoided him, never appearing in his presence. He often used to say, "I am only a magician; Yun is a God."

One day, Chon told his wife that Yun would come that afternoon to try to kill him. "And so," he said, "I will change my shape to escape his clutches. If anyone comes asking for me, just say that I am not at home." He then transformed <note>original: "metamorphosed"</note> himself into a beetle and crawled under a large earthenware jar <note>original: "crock"; likely an *onggi*, a traditional Korean storage jar</note> that stood overturned in the courtyard.

Chon’s wife, seeing such a pretty woman arrive and ask for her husband in such a familiar way, flew into a rage. She thought, “That rascal obviously has a second wife he never told me about! Everything he just told me was a lie.” In a fury, she went out and smashed the large earthenware jar <note>original: "crock"; likely an *onggi*, a traditional Korean jar used for fermenting or storing food</note> with a club. When the jar was broken, there was the beetle underneath it. Then the woman who had visited suddenly changed into a bee; she flew at the beetle and stung it. Chon, reverting <note>original: "metamorphosed"</note> to his natural form, collapsed and died, and the bee flew away.

Yun lived at his own house as usual when he suddenly burst into tears one day. His family, alarmed, asked him the reason.

He replied, “My sister, who lives in Jeolla Province <note>original: "Chulla Province"; one of the traditional eight provinces of Korea, located in the southwest</note>, has died at this very moment.” He then called his servants and had them prepare funeral supplies, saying, “Her family is poor where she lives, and so I must help them.”

Take this letter and go at once. I shall expect you back tonight with the answer. The matter is of such great importance that if you do not bring it as I order, and within the time appointed, I shall have you punished."

He replied, "I shall be in time; do not be anxious."

Yun then gave him the letter and the bundle <note>referring to the funeral supplies mentioned on the previous page</note>, and he went outside the main gateway and disappeared.

Before dark, he returned with the answer. The letter read: "She died at such an hour today and we were in a desperate situation <note>original: "in straits"</note> regarding what to do, when your letter arrived with the supplies, just as though we had seen each other in person. It is wonderful!" The man who brought the answer immediately went out and disappeared. The house of mourning was located over a ten-day journey from Seoul, but he returned before sunset, in the space of only two or three hours.

### VI

## THE WILD-CAT WOMAN

[KIM SU-IK <note>Kim Su-ik (1598–1636) was a scholar-official during the Joseon Dynasty known for his integrity and loyalty.</note> was a native of Seoul who passed his preliminary civil service exams <note>original: "matriculated"</note> in 1624 and passed the highest level of the exams <note>original: "graduated"</note> in 1630. In 1636, when the King fled to the mountain fortress of Namhansanseong <note>original: "Nam-han"; the fortress where King Injo took refuge during the Second Manchu Invasion of Korea</note> to escape the invading Manchu army, Kim Su-ik accompanied him. He opposed any surrender to the Qing Dynasty <note>original: "China"</note> or any treaty with them; however, because his advice was not accepted, he withdrew from public life.]

[Tong Chung-so <note>original: "Tong Chung-so"; refers to Dong Zhongshu (179–104 BC), a renowned Chinese philosopher and scholar of the Han Dynasty.</note> was a Chinese man of great fame. He once desired to devote himself entirely to study and did not leave his room for three years. During this time, a young man called on him one day, and while he stood waiting, the visitor said to himself, "It will rain today." Tong replied at once, "If you are not a fox, you are a wild cat <note>original: "wild cat"; in East Asian folklore, animals like foxes and leopard cats were believed to be shapeshifters who could take human form but often betrayed their animal instincts</note>—get out of here!" and the man immediately ran away. How Tong came to know this was from the proverb: "Birds that live in the trees know when the wind will blow; beasts that live in the ground know when it is going to rain." The wild cat had unconsciously revealed his true identity.]

### *The Story*

The former magistrate of Quelpart <note>original: "Quelpart"; the historical name for Jeju Island, South Korea</note>, Kim Su-ik, lived inside the South Gate of Seoul. When he was young, it was his habit to study Classical Chinese <note>original: "study Chinese"; referring to the study of Classical Chinese texts, which was the standard education for the Korean elite (yangban) during the Joseon Dynasty</note> every day until late at night. Once, when feeling hungry, he called for his wife to bring him something to eat.

His wife replied, “We have nothing in the house except seven or eight chestnuts. Shall I roast these and bring them to you?”

Kim replied, “Good; bring them.”

The servants were asleep, and there was no one available to answer his call, so the wife went to the kitchen, made a fire, and cooked them herself. Meanwhile, Kim waited for her to return.

After a little while, she brought them in a small basket, cooked and ready to be served. Kim ate and enjoyed them very much. Meanwhile, she sat before his desk and waited. Suddenly, the door opened, and another person entered. Kim raised his eyes to see, and there was the exact duplicate of his wife, with a basket in her hand and roasted chestnuts. As he looked at both of them by the light, the two women were identical copies <note>original: "perfect facsimiles"</note> of each other. The two women also looked back and forth at one another in alarm, saying, “What is this that has happened? Who are you?”

Kim once again received the roasted nuts, laid

<summary>While studying late at night, Kim Su-ik is visited by his wife with a snack of roasted chestnuts, only for a second, identical version of his wife to enter the room moments later with the exact same offering.</summary>
<keywords>Kim Su-ik, Seoul, shape-shifting, doppelgänger, chestnuts, Joseon folklore, domestic supernatural</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Kim Su-ik and the supernatural intruder who took the form of his wife. Having grabbed both women to see which would reveal its true nature at dawn, the narrative reaches its climax.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>

<header>THE WILD-CAT WOMAN</header>

Kim laid the chestnuts down and then took a firm hold of each woman—the first one by her right hand and the second by her left—holding fast until the break of day.

At last the roosters crowed, and the eastern sky began to lighten. The woman whose right hand he held said, “Why do you hold me like this? It hurts; let me go!” She shook and tugged, but Kim held all the tighter. After a short struggle, she fell to the floor and suddenly changed into a wild cat <note>original: "wild cat"; in Korean folklore, cats were often seen as mysterious or even sinister creatures capable of supernatural feats, though less commonly than foxes</note>. Kim, in fear and surprise, let her go, and she escaped through the door. What a pity that he did not secure the beast once and for all!

**Note by the author**—Stories of foxes turning into women and deceiving people are told in the *Guangji* <note>original: "Kwang-keui"; referring to the *Taiping Guangji* (Extensive Records of the Taiping Era), a massive 10th-century Chinese collection of supernatural stories and legends</note> and other Chinese novels, but the wild cat’s transformation is more wonderful still, and something that I have never heard of before. By what law of nature do creatures like foxes and wild cats change in this way? I am unable to find any law that explains it. Some say that the fox carries a magic charm with which it performs these feats, but can this also account for the wild cat?

->IM BANG.<-

A certain scribe of Chungcheong Province <note>original: "Chung-chong Province"; one of the traditional eight provinces of Korea, located in the center of the country</note>, named Kim Gyeong-jin, once told me the following story. I asked what had happened to them, and they finally replied, 'We were eating our noon meal by the side of the road when a Buddhist monk <note>original: "priest"; during the Joseon Dynasty, the era in which this story is set, Buddhism was often suppressed by the state, and monks were frequently treated with low social status by the Confucian scholar-elite</note> came by. He was a proud, arrogant fellow who refused to bow or show any recognition of us. One of the servants, offended by this, shouted at him. The monk, however, beat him with his staff, and when the others went to help, he beat them also, so that they were completely overpowered <note>original: "worsted"</note> and unable to rise or walk. He then scolded the scholar, saying, "You did not reprimand your servants for their insult to me, so I will have to take it out on you as well." The Buddhist then gave him a number of vicious

He then dismounted, drew his sword, pierced both of the monk’s hands and passed a string through them, tied him to his horse’s tail, and returned triumphantly to where the scholar lay. He said, ‘Now, do with this fellow as you please. I am leaving.’ ”

### VIII

## THE VISION OF THE HOLY MAN

Yi Chi-Ham (known as Master To-jong <note>original: "Yi Chi-Ham (Master To-jong)"; Yi Chi-ham (1517–1578) was a famous Korean scholar, philosopher, and diviner known for his eccentricity and his supposed authorship of a book of prophecy called the *Tojong-gyeok*.</note>). A story is told of him that on the day after his wedding, he went out wearing his *topo* <note>original: "topo"; a traditional Korean overcoat worn by scholars and officials during the Joseon Dynasty.</note> (a ceremonial coat), but he returned later without it. When people asked what had happened, they found that he had torn it into pieces to serve as bandages for a sick child he had encountered during his walk.

Once, he had a premonition <note>original: "an impression"</note> that his father-in-law’s home would soon be struck by a great disaster. Consequently, he took his wife and fled the area. In the following year, due to a political offense, the household was indeed wiped out and the family was completely destroyed.

Master To-jong was not only a prophet but also a magician, as was shown by his skill in handling a boat. When he took to the sea, the waters became calm before him, and his entire journey was peaceful. He would sometimes be gone for a year or more, traveling to many different parts of the world.

He practiced fasting and would sometimes go for months without eating. He also overcame—

thirst, and in the hot days of summer, he would avoid drinking. He suppressed all pain and suffering, so that when he walked and his feet were blistered, he paid no attention to it.

While young, he was a disciple of a famous Taoist <note>Taoism is a Chinese philosophy and religion focused on living in harmony with the "Tao" (the Way), often associated with nature and mysticism.</note>, So Wha-dam <note>original: "So Wha-dam"; also known as Seo Gyeong-deok (1489–1546), a renowned Neo-Confucian philosopher and scholar.</note>. As his follower, he used to dress in grass cloth (the poor man’s clothing), wear straw shoes, and carry his bundle on his back. He was on familiar terms with ministers of state, and yet he showed indifference to their greatness and pomp. He was acquainted with various magic practices, so that when boating, he used to hang out gourd cups at each corner of the boat. Thus equipped, he traveled many times to and from Quelpart <note>original: "Quelpart"; the historical European name for Jeju Island, located off the southern coast of South Korea.</note> and never encountered a storm <note>original: "met a wind"</note>. He engaged in trade, made money, and bought land that yielded several thousand bags of rice, which he distributed among the poor.

He lived in Seoul in a small dugout, so that his name became “Mud Pavilion,” or To-jong <note>original: "To-jong"; Yi Chi-ham's pen name literally means "earthen pavilion" or "mud hut," reflecting his humble living conditions.</note>. His cap was made of metal, which he used to cook his food in; he would سپس wash it and put it back on his head. He also used to wear wooden shoes and ride on a pack saddle <note>A simple frame for a horse or ox to carry loads, rather than a comfortable passenger saddle.</note>.

<header>THE VISION OF THE HOLY MAN</header>
<sig>E</sig>

He flourished in this role. When anyone had no special skill, he had them weave straw shoes. He encouraged them until they could make as many as ten pairs a day.

Yul-gok <note>original: "Yul-gok"; Yi I (1536–1584), one of Korea's most famous Neo-Confucian scholars and a contemporary of To-jong.</note> said of him that he was a dreamer and not suited for this matter-of-fact world, because he belonged to the realm of flowers and beautiful birds, songs and sweet breezes, and not to the common life <note>original: "common clay"</note> of grain, beef, and radishes. To-jong heard this and replied, “Even if I am not the same quality as beans and corn, I still rank with acorns and chestnuts. Why am I considered completely useless?”

## THE RECORDS OF KOREA’S FAMOUS MEN.

### *The Story*

Master To-jong was once a merchant, and in his trading, he traveled as far as the East Sea <note>original: "East Sea"; the body of water between Korea and Japan, often called the Sea of Japan.</note>. One night, he slept in a fishing village on the shore. At that time, another stranger arrived who was said to be an <term>i-in</term> or “holy man” <note>original: "i-in"; a person with supernatural or extraordinary powers, often living as a recluse.</note>. The three of them met and talked until late at night—the master of the house, the “holy man,” and To-jong. The night was very clear and beautifully calm. The “holy man” looked out over the vast expanse of water for a time, then suddenly jumped with terror and said, “An awful thing is about to happen.”

His companions, alarmed by his behavior, asked him what he meant. He replied, “In two hours or so, there will be a tidal wave that will engulf this...

<page-num>50</page-num>

->KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES<-

...the whole village, utterly destroying everything. If you do not hurry to escape, you will all be like fish caught in a net <note>original: "as fish in a net"; a metaphor for being trapped with no hope of escape.</note>."

To-jong, being something of an astrologer <note>original: "astrologer"; in this context, someone skilled in "Saju" or "Myeongni," the traditional study of fate based on the stars, elements, and the I Ching.</note> himself, first tried to solve the mystery behind this warning, but he could find no explanation for it.

The owner of the house did not believe the warning and refused to prepare for their escape.

The "holy man" said, however, "Even if you do not believe what I say, let us go a short way up the face of the mountain behind us. If my words prove false, we can simply come back down, and no one will be any the worse for it. If you still do not wish to trust me, leave your goods and furniture exactly as they are and let the people come away."

To-jong was greatly interested, although he could not understand the situation. The master of the house could also no longer refuse this proposal, so he took his family and a few light belongings and followed the "holy man" up the hill.

He had them climb to the very top, "in order," he said, "to escape."

To-jong did not go all the way to the top, but instead sat down about halfway up the mountain. He asked the "holy man" if he would be safe enough there.

The "holy man" replied, "Others would never escape if they remained where you are, but you will only receive a scare and will survive it."

# THE VISION OF THE HOLY MAN

At dawn, as the rooster crowed <note>original: "cock-crow"; the traditional way of marking the beginning of the day in a time before clocks.</note>, the sea suddenly rose up and overflowed its banks. The waves surged toward the heavens, rushing up the mountainsides until they reached To-jong’s feet. The entire town on the seashore was swallowed by the water. When morning light arrived, the flood pulled back.

To-jong bowed to the "holy man" and asked if he could become his student <note>original: "disciple"; a common request in these tales when someone encounters a person with supernatural insight.</note>. The "holy man," however, denied having any special wisdom, claiming that he had simply discovered the danger by chance. He was a person who did not boast about his own spiritual achievements. To-jong asked where the man lived, and he pointed to a place nearby. To-jong then departed, but when he went to look for him the next day, the house was empty, and the man was nowhere to be found.

### IX
## THE VISIT OF THE DIVINE BEING <note>original: "Man of God"; in this context, it refers to a *Sin-in*, a supernatural or godly person from Korean mythology.</note>

In the thirty-third year of the Wanli Emperor <note>original: "Mal-yok"; the Wanli Emperor of the Chinese Ming Dynasty (reigned 1572–1620).</note> of the Ming Dynasty (1605 AD)—which was the year *Eulsa* <note>The 42nd year of the 60-year sexagenary cycle used in the traditional Korean calendar.</note> of the reign of King Seonjo <note>original: "Son-jo"; the fourteenth king of the Joseon Dynasty.</note>—in the seventh lunar month, a great rain fell. It was such a rain as had not been seen since the founding of the dynasty. Before that rain began, a man from Gangwon <note>original: "Kang-won"; a mountainous province in eastern Korea.</note> Province was cutting wood on a hillside. While he was working, a celestial being <note>original: "angel"</note> in golden armor, riding a white horse and carrying a spear, came down to him from heaven. His appearance was most dazzling, and the woodcutter, looking at him, recognized him as a divine being. A Buddhist priest carrying a staff also descended in his entourage <note>original: "train"; meaning a group of followers or attendants.</note>. The priest's appearance was also very remarkable.

The divine being stopped his horse and seemed to be talking with the priest, while the woodcutter, alarmed by the magnificent sight, hid himself among the trees.

The divine being seemed to be very angry for some reason. He raised his spear and, pointing in all four directions <note>original: "to the four winds"</note>, said, "I shall flood all the

<vocab>Wanli Emperor (Mal-yok), Ming Dynasty, Eulsa, Seonjo (Son-jo), Gangwon (Kang-won) Province, celestial being (angel), Buddhist priest, divine being (Man of God), woodcutter</vocab>

<summary>In 1605, a woodcutter in Gangwon Province witnesses a dazzling divine warrior and a Buddhist priest descend from heaven. The warrior, appearing angry, threatens to flood the world just before a record-breaking rainfall begins.</summary>
<keywords>King Seonjo, Wanli Emperor, Gangwon Province, divine encounter, celestial warrior, 1605 flood, Korean mythology</keywords>

<page-num>53</page-num>

<header>THE VISIT OF THE DIVINE BEING 53</header>

the earth from this point to that point, and destroy its inhabitants."

The priest following him cried out and begged him to stop, saying, "This will mean total destruction for mortals; please, let your anger fall on me instead." As he prayed so earnestly, the Divine Being <note>original: "Man of God"; in Korean mythology, this likely refers to a *Sin-in*, a celestial deity or supernatural person.</note> spoke again: "Then I will limit the destruction to these specific places. Will that suffice?"

But the priest prayed even more earnestly, until the Divine Being replied firmly, "I have already reduced the punishment by more than half for your sake; I can do no more." Although the priest continued to pray, the Divine Being refused him, so at last the priest submissively said, "Your will be done."

They finished their conversation and both departed, passing through the upper air and returning to heaven.

The two had talked for a long time, but because there was a considerable distance between them and the woodcutter, he did not hear everything they said clearly.

However, he hurried home and escaped with his wife and family. From 그날 (that day) on, the rain began to fall. During the storm, Mount Odae <note>original: "Otai"; Mount Odaesan, a sacred mountain in Gangwon Province.</note> collapsed, and the earth beneath it sank until it became a vast lake. All the inhabitants were destroyed, and only the woodcutter and his family made their escape.

## X

# THE LITERARY MAN OF IMSIL

[**The** summoning of spirits is one of the powers believed to be held by the followers of Laozi <note>original: "the Old Philosopher"; Laozi is the legendary founder of Taoism.</note>, who reach a high state of spiritual achievement. While natural human desires remain, they cloud and block spiritual vision; once rid of them, even celestial spirits and immortal beings are revealed to one's sight. It is said, "If once all the distractions of the physical body are removed, even the Supreme Deity <note>original: "God"; in this context, the author refers to the Jade Emperor or *Sangje*, the ruler of heaven in East Asian mythology.</note> can be seen." They also say, "If I have no selfish desire, the night around me will shine with golden light; and if all harmful thoughts are truly put away, the wild deer of the mountain will come down and play beside me."]

Ha Sa-gong, a Taoist <term>Taoist: a follower of Taoism, a philosophy and religion emphasizing harmony with the "Tao" (the Way) and often seeking immortality or spiritual transcendence.</term> of great achievement, as an old man used to go fishing, and flocks of pigeons would land on his head and shoulders. Returning home one day, he told his wife that there were so many birds they bothered him. "Why not catch one of them?" his wife asked. "Catch one?" he said. "What would you do with it?" "Why, eat it, of course." So on the second day Ha went out...

### *The Story*

In the year 1654, there was a scholar <note>original: "man of letters"</note> living in Imsil who claimed that he could control spirits, and that two demon guards were constantly at his command. One day, he was sitting with a friend playing chess, and they agreed that the loser of each game was to pay a penalty in drinks. The friend lost but refused to pay his wager, so the master said, “If you do not pay up, I’ll make things difficult for you.” 

The man, however, continued to refuse, until at last the master, losing his patience, turned his back on him and suddenly shouted a magic formula into the air as if he were giving a command. The man dashed into the courtyard to make his escape, but an unseen hand stripped him and administered such a series of loud, heavy blows that they left dark, bruised marks on his skin. Unable to bear the pain any longer, he gave in; then the master laughed and let him go.

On another occasion, he was sitting with a friend while a shaman's <note>original: "witch"</note> <term>*gut*: an exorcism ceremony or ritual performed by a Korean shaman (mudang) to interact with the spirit world, often involving music, dancing, and food offerings.</term> was taking place in the neighboring village, with drums and—

He looked so strange and threatening that his friend asked what was wrong. For the time being, the scholar gave no answer, and they simply continued on their journey.

## XI

# THE SOLDIER OF GANGHWA <note>original: "Kang-wha"; modern: Ganghwa Island, an island off the west coast of South Korea</note>

[In Eastern tradition, it is believed that the air is full of invisible elements that, once harnessed and controlled, can take on various forms of life. The man from Ganghwa had mastered the art of gathering the necessary elements to create a butterfly. This practice originates from Taoism and is known as *seonsul* <note>original: "son-sul"</note>, or Taoist magic.]

### *The Story*

There was once a soldier from Ganghwa who was the headman of his village. He was a man of low social standing and appeared to have no special talents. One day, his wife—overcome by a fit of jealousy—sat sewing in her private inner room. It was mid-winter, and he was required to stay at home; so, hoping to cheer her up and distract her from her bad mood, he asked her, "Would you like to see me create some butterflies?"

His wife, becoming even angrier at this, scolded him for his boldness <note>original: "impudence"</note> and ignored him.

The soldier then took her sewing basket and selected scraps of silk of various colors from it. He tucked...

<summary>This page introduces a seemingly ordinary soldier from Ganghwa who claims to be able to perform Taoist magic by creating butterflies from scraps of silk.</summary>
<keywords>Ganghwa, Taoism, seonsul, magic, butterflies, folk tale, marriage</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of the soldier from Ganghwa who, in the previous page, began to demonstrate his magical abilities to his angry wife by using scraps of silk.</meta>

<page-num>59</page-num>

<header>THE SOLDIER OF GANGHWA</header>

...them into his palm, closed his hand over them, and repeated a prayer, after which he threw the handful into the air. Immediately, beautiful butterflies filled the room, dazzling the eyes and shining in all the colors of the silk itself.

The wife, mystified by the wonder of it, forgot her anger. A little later, the soldier opened his hand and held it up, and they all flew back into it. He closed it tight and then opened his hand again, and they were only pieces of silk once more. His wife alone saw this; it was unknown to others. No such strange magic had ever been heard of before.

In 1637, when Ganghwa <note>original: "Kang-wha"</note> fell to the Manchus <note>This refers to the Second Manchu Invasion of Korea, which resulted in the Qing Dynasty establishing a tributary relationship with the Joseon Kingdom</note>, all the people of the area fled, crying for their lives. Meanwhile, the soldier remained undisturbed at his home, eating his meals with his wife and family just as usual. He laughed at the neighbors hurrying by. He said, "The barbarians will not touch this town; why are you running like that?" As it turned out, while the entire island was devastated, the soldier's village was spared.

->IM BANG.<-

### XII

## CURSED BY THE SNAKE

[Ha Yeon <note>original: "HA YON" (1376–1453). He was a famous scholar-official during the early Joseon Dynasty</note> passed his civil service examinations <note>original: "graduated"</note> in the year 1396 and became the magistrate of Anak County. He built many pavilions in and around his official residence where people could rest. As he traveled through his district and saw the farmers working hard, he wrote many songs and verses to encourage them in their labor. Later, he became a royal censor, and King Taejong <note>original: "Tai-jong"</note> praised him, saying, “Well done, good and faithful servant.” Eventually, he rose to the position of Chief Justice <note>term: Chief Justice; this likely refers to his role as a high-ranking official in the State Council or the Office of the Inspector General</note>. He cleared the public offices of all corrupt officials and purified the Royal Court. Whenever he had leisure time, it was his habit to dress in ceremonial robes, burn incense, sit with disciplined attention, and write prayer verses all day long.

When he was young, while serving in the Court of the Crown Prince, he wrote a verse that was praised with the comment: “Beautiful writing, beautiful thought; truly a treasure.” He was a dedicated student and a great seeker of knowledge, as well as a loyal and lovable friend. As a boy, he studied under the patriot Jeong Mong-ju <note>original: "Cheung Mong-ju"; a famous scholar and the last great loyalist of the Goryeo Dynasty</note>, and he was upright and pure in all

He eventually rose to the rank of Prime Minister <note>original: "Prime Minister"; likely referring to the position of Chief State Councillor (Yeonguijeong)</note>.]

### *The Story*

The ancestral home of Prince Ha Yeon <note>original: "Ha Yun"</note> was in Geumcheon <note>original: "Keum-chon"</note> County. He was a renowned Minister of State during an era of peace and prosperity, and he frequently spent his leisure time at his summer house in that same county. It was a large, well-maintained mansion that remained occupied by his descendants for many years after his death.

The local people told a very strange story regarding Ha and the source of his prosperity, which goes like this: He had kept a large earthenware jar <note>original: "crock"</note> in an upper room, which was used for storing flour. One day, a servant went to get some flour and lifted the lid, only for a massive snake to suddenly appear from the depths of the jar. Startled and terrified, the servant scrambled back and informed the master. The master sent his servants <note>original: "men-slaves"</note> to bring the jar downstairs. ## XIII
# THE MAN ON THE ROAD

During the Manchu War of 1636 <note>also known as the Second Manchu Invasion of Korea, when the Qing dynasty invaded the Joseon kingdom</note>, the people of Seoul fled in crowds to make their escape. One group of refugees suddenly came upon a massive force of the enemy, who were armed and on horseback. The hills and valleys seemed to be filled with them, and there was no possible way to escape. They did not know what to do. In the midst of their confusion, they suddenly saw someone sitting peacefully in the middle of the main roadway just ahead, underneath a pine tree, looking completely unconcerned. He had dismounted from his horse, which a servant held while standing close by. A screen made of several yards of cotton cloth was hanging up just in front of him, as if to shield him from the dust of the passing army.

The people who were trying to escape came up to this stranger and said imploringly, “We are all doomed to die. What should we do?”

The mysterious stranger said, “Why should you die? And why are you so frightened? Sit down next to me and watch the barbarians <note>original: "barbarians," a term historically used by Koreans to refer to the Manchu invaders</note> go by.”

<vocab>Manchu War, 1636, Seoul, barbarians, roadway, pine tree</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces a story set during the Manchu War of 1636, where a group of panicked refugees encounters a mysterious, calm stranger sitting in the middle of the invasion route.</summary>
<keywords>Manchu War, 1636, Seoul, invasion, mysterious stranger, refugees, Korean folklore</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of "The Man on the Road" during the Manchu invasion of 1636. The mysterious, calm stranger has just invited a group of terrified refugees to sit with him as the enemy passes.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

The people, seeing how calm he was and that he showed no fear, and realizing they had no other way to escape, did as he commanded <note>original: "bade"</note> and sat down.

The enemy cavalry rode past in huge numbers, killing everyone they encountered—not a single person escaped. Yet, when they reached the spot where the magician <note>original: "magician," referring to someone practicing supernatural or Daoist arts</note> sat, they passed by without apparently seeing a thing. They continued like this until evening, by which time the entire army had moved on. The stranger and the people with him sat there all day without any harm coming to them, even though they were effectively in the middle of the enemy’s camp.

Finally realizing that he possessed wonderful magic, the people all came together and bowed before him, asking for his name and where he lived. However, he did not answer. instead, he mounted his beautiful horse and rode away so swiftly that no one could catch him.

The next day, the group met a man who had been captured but had managed to escape. They asked him if he had seen anything unusual the day before. He replied, "When I was following the barbarian <note>the Manchu invaders</note> army and passed a certain point"—he indicated the exact spot where the magician had sat with the people—"we skirted along great walls and steep cliffs..."

<summary>The mysterious stranger uses magic to hide himself and the refugees from the passing Manchu army; a later witness reveals that the enemy saw the group not as people, but as a fortified wall.</summary>

## THE OLD MAN WHO BECAME A FISH

A few years ago, a prominent official became the magistrate of Kosong <note>Kosong (Goseong) is a county in Gangwon Province, now divided by the border between North and South Korea; it is famous for its coastal scenery.</note> County. One day, a guest visited him to pay his respects, and when noon arrived, the magistrate had a meal prepared for him, which included a dish of skate <note>A type of cartilaginous fish similar to a ray; it is a traditional delicacy in Korean cuisine, often served fermented or in soups.</note> soup. When the guest saw the soup, his face contorted <note>original: "twisted his features"</note> and he refused it, saying, “Today I am fasting from meat, and so I beg to be excused.” His face grew very pale, and tears flowed from his eyes. The magistrate thought this behavior was strange and asked him several times for the meaning of it. When he could no longer avoid giving a reply, he went into all the details and told him the story.

“Your humble servant,” <note>original: "Your humble servant"—a traditional, self-deprecating way of referring to oneself (like the pronoun "I") when speaking to a social superior.</note> he said, “has experienced many unheard-of and unhappy things in his life, which he has never told to a living soul. But now that your Excellency asks it of me, I cannot refrain from telling you. My father was a very old man, nearly a hundred, when one day he was struck down with a high fever, in which his body was like a fiery furnace. Seeing the danger he was in, his...”

We protested <note>original: "remonstrated"</note> and begged him not to do it, but he grew very angry and said, "If you do not do as I command, you will be the death of me." When he saw the water, he was delighted and said, "This clear, flowing water is curing my illness." We protested again, but he became so furiously angry that we were helpless. We feared that if we insisted on staying, his condition would worsen, so we were forced to give in. We went a short distance away and then turned back to look, only to find that our father had suddenly vanished from the place where he had been sitting. We rushed back to see what had happened. <header>XV</header>

## THE GEOMANCER

[Yi Eui-sin was an expert in geomancy <note>Known in Korea as *Pung-su*, geomancy is the practice of positioning houses or graves to harmonize with the spiritual energy of the land.</note>. His craft evidently emerged as a by-product of Taoism, but it also incorporates elements of ancient Chinese philosophy. The Positive and the Negative <note>original: "The Two Primary Principles in Nature," referring to Yin and Yang.</note> play a major role, as do the Five Elements: Metal, Wood, Water, Fire, and Earth. When selecting a site, a location for a house is called a "male" choice, while a gravesite is called a "female" choice.

Huge sums of money have been spent in Korea on geomancers and their associates in the hope of finding lucky homes for the living and favorable resting places for the dead. The Korean belief is that, in some mysterious way, all of our fortune is connected to Mother Earth.]

### *The Story*

There was once a geomancer named Yi Eui-sin who, while searching for a specific mountain vein <note>In geomancy, "veins" (*ryong*) are spiritual energy pathways through the landscape, similar to pulses or nerves in a human body.</note>, began at the Dragon Ridge in North Hamgyong Province and followed it as far as Pine Mountain in Yangju.

<summary>This page introduces the practice of Korean geomancy (Pung-su) and begins the story of Yi Eui-sin, a master practitioner tracking a spiritual mountain vein across the country.</summary>
<keywords>Yi Eui-sin, geomancy, Pung-su, Taoism, Five Elements, Yin and Yang, North Hamgyong Province, Yangju, mountain veins</keywords>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

County, where it stopped in a beautifully rounded end, forming a perfect site for burial. After wandering all day in the hills, Yi’s hungry spirit cried out for food. He saw a small house beneath the hill, went to it, and knocking at the door, asked for something to eat. A mourner <note>In traditional Korea, mourners wore distinct clothing made of coarse hemp to signify their grief and status.</note>, who had recently suffered a loss, came out in a respectful and kindly way and gave him a dish of white gruel <note>original: "white gruel"; likely *juk*, a simple rice porridge.</note>.

After he had eaten, Yi asked how long the man had been a mourner and if he had already held the funeral. The owner answered, “I am just now entering the period of full mourning, but we have not yet arranged for the funeral.” He spoke in a sad and disheartened way.

Yi felt sorry for him and asked the reason. “I wonder if it is because you are poor that you have not yet made the necessary arrangements, or perhaps you have not yet found a suitable site? I am an expert in reading the hills <note>original: "reading the hills"; the practice of geomancy or *Pung-su*, which interprets the spiritual energy of the landscape.</note>, and I will tell you of a site; would you care to see it?”

The mourner thanked him most gratefully and said, “I would be delighted to know of it.”

Yi then showed him the end of the great mountain vein <note>original: "vein"; a *ryong* or "dragon" line of auspicious energy in the earth.</note> that he had just discovered, as well as the specific spot for the grave and how to orient it using compass points. “After possessing this site,” he said, “you will be greatly enriched, but in ten years you will have cause to arrange for another site. When that time comes to...”

<summary>While searching for a burial site, the geomancer Yi Eui-sin meets a poor mourner and offers him an auspicious gravesite that promises wealth for ten years.</summary>
