"When those years pass, please call for me, won't you? In calling for me, just ask for Yi So-pang <note>original: "Yi So-pang"; *Sŏ-bang* was a title used for a married man or a scholar of modest rank.</note>, who lives in the West School Ward of Seoul."

The mourner did as he was directed, and just as the geomancer had foretold, all his affairs prospered. He built a large house with a tiled roof <note>A tiled roof signaled wealth and status in old Korea, as commoners lived in thatched-roof cottages.</note> and decorated the grave with great stone monuments, as a prosperous and high-minded country gentleman should do.

Ten years later, a guest called one day. After greeting the owner, he asked, "Is that grave over there, beyond the stream, yours?" 

The master answered, "It is mine." 

Then the stranger said, "That is a famous site, but ten years have passed since you took possession of it, and the luck is gone. Why do you not move it? If you wait too long, you will regret it and may meet with a great disaster."

Hearing this, the owner thought of Yi the geomancer and what he had said years before. Remembering that advice, he asked the stranger to remain as his guest while he went the next day to Seoul to look for Yi in the West School Ward. He found him and told him why he had come.

Yi said, "I already knew about this." 

So the two traveled together back to the owner's home. Once there, they went up the hill with the visitor. Yi asked the guest, "Why did you tell the master to change the site?"

The guest replied, "This hill is a Kneeling

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

"...Pheasant formation. If the pheasant kneels too long, it cannot endure it, so within a limited time, it must fly away. Ten years is that limit; that is why I spoke as I did."

Yi laughed and said, “Your perspective is only a partial view. You have thought of only one thing, but there are other conditions involved.” Then he pointed to the peak in the rear and said, “Over there is Dog Hill.” Pointing to the one below, he said, “That is Falcon Hill.” Finally, pointing to the stream in front, he said, “That is Cat River. This is the complete group: the dog is behind, the falcon is just above, and the cat is in front. How, then, can the pheasant fly? It does not dare to move.” <note>In Korean geomancy (*pungsu*), landforms are often named after animals. Here, Yi explains that while a "Pheasant" site might normally lose its luck after ten years, the presence of its natural predators—the dog, falcon, and cat—frightens the pheasant into staying put, thereby locking the good fortune in place forever.</note>

The guest replied, “Teacher, your eyes are truly enlightened; you see much further than ordinary men.”

From that day forward, the Yi family of Pine Hill became a great and famous lineage.

## THE MAN WHO BECAME A PIG

[**Kim Yu** was the son of a provincial magistrate who passed the highest civil service examinations <note>original: "graduated with literary honours"</note> in 1596. In 1623, he was one of the loyal courtiers who joined forces to dethrone the tyrant <note>original: "wicked"</note> Prince Gwanghaegun <note>original: "Kwang-hai"</note> and place King Injo on the throne. He was raised to the rank of Prince and later became the Prime Minister. In the year 1624, when Yi Gwal <note>original: "Yi Kwal"</note> led an insurrection, Kim Yu was the one responsible for suppressing it and bringing many of the rebels to justice. In 1648, he died at the age of seventy-seven.

During the final year of King Seonjo’s <note>original: "Son-jo"</note> reign, the King gathered his grandchildren together and had them practice calligraphy <note>original: "write Chinese"</note> and draw pictures for him. At that time, the future King Injo was only a little boy, and he drew a picture of a horse. King Seonjo gave the drawing to the scholar-official Yi Hang-bok, but when Yi went into exile some years later, he gave the picture to Kim Yu. Kim Yu accepted it and hung it in his house, where it remained for many years.

One day, while King Injo was traveling outside the Palace, he was caught in a heavy rainstorm, and—]

On examining it carefully, he recognized it as the picture he had drawn when he was a boy, and he wondered how it could have come to be there.

I looked out, and truly, it was the man I had seen in my dream! So, I have treated him with the best hospitality I could offer.”

[Kim Yu] became from that time on the faithful supporter of Prince Injo, and later helped to place him on the throne.

After Injo became king, he privately asked Kim Yu where he had obtained the picture. Kim Yu replied, “I received it from Prince Yi Hang-bok.” <note>Yi Hang-bok (1556–1618) was a renowned scholar-official and Chief State Councilor known for his wit and his role in the Imjin War.</note>

Kim Yu then sent for Yi’s son and asked him how his father had acquired it. The son explained, “During the final year of King Seonjo’s <note>original: "Son-jo," the fourteenth king of the Joseon Dynasty and grandfather to Injo</note> reign, he called my father and all his grandchildren together to show them the writings and drawings of the young princes. My father examined them with great interest, but the King gave him only one as a keepsake: the drawing of the horse.” The picture depicted a willow tree with a horse tied to it. Kim Yu then understood the hidden meaning behind the gift: King Seonjo had intended for Yi Hang-bok to support Injo’s succession to the throne.

### *The Story*

One day, the son of this patriarch <note>the elderly head of the family</note> came to the Minister’s office and requested an audience. Kim ordered him to be admitted and asked about...

[The Minister asked] why he had come. The man said, “I have something very important to say, a private matter to present to your Excellency. Since there are so many guests with you now, I will return in the evening to tell you.”

In the evening, when all the guests had left, he returned. The Minister dismissed his personal retainers <term>attendants or guards in a noble household</term> and asked the purpose of his visit. The man replied, “My father, though very old, was—as you may know—a strong and healthy man. One day, he called us children to him and said, ‘I wish to take a siesta <note>The translator uses "siesta" here to refer to a traditional Korean afternoon nap.</note>, so close the door and all of you leave the room. Do not let anyone venture in until I call you.’”

“We children agreed, of course, and obeyed. However, by late at night, there had been no call or command to open the door, and we began to grow anxious. At last, we peered through a crack in the door, and to our surprise, there was our father changed into a huge pig! Terrified by the sight, we opened the door and looked in; the animal grunted, growled, and rushed to get past us. We hurriedly closed the door again and held a consultation <term>a formal discussion or meeting</term>."

“Some said, ‘Let’s keep the pig just as it is, inside the house, and take care of it.’ Others said, ‘Let’s hold a funeral and bury it.’ Since we are just uneducated country people and did not know what to do in such strange circumstances, I have come to ask your advice.”

<meta>This page concludes the advice given by Minister Kim (Prince Kim) to the son of the man who transformed into a pig, and describes the son's eventual actions.</meta>

<header>THE MAN WHO BECAME A PIG</header>

"...your Excellency. Please think over this startling phenomenon and tell us what we ought to do."

Prince Kim, hearing this, was greatly startled. He thought it over for a long time and at last said, "No such mysterious thing has ever been heard of before, and I really do not know what is best to do under these circumstances. However, it seems to me that since this transformation <note>original: "metamorphosis"</note> has occurred, you should not bury the creature while it is still alive, so abandon the idea of a funeral. Furthermore, since it is no longer a human being, I do not think it is right to keep it in the house. You say that it wants to escape, and as a cave in the woods or hills is its natural home, I think you should take it out and let it go free into the trackless depths of some mountainous country, where no human has ever set foot."

The son accepted this wise counsel and did as the Minister advised; he took the creature into the deep mountains and let it go. Then he put on sackcloth <term>coarse, uncomfortable clothing worn as a traditional sign of mourning</term>, mourned, buried his father's clothes in place of a body for the funeral, and observed the anniversary of the transformation as the day for the sacrificial ceremony <note>This refers to the *jesa*, a traditional Korean memorial rite performed to honor one's ancestors.</note>.

## XVII

# THE OLD WOMAN WHO BECAME A GOBLIN <note>original: "goblin"; in Korean folklore, this likely refers to a *dokkaebi*, a nature spirit or creature with supernatural powers that can be both mischievous and helpful.</note>

There was once a Confucian scholar <note>A man belonging to the educated elite of the Joseon Dynasty who studied the teachings of Confucius to prepare for government service.</note> who lived in the southern part of Seoul. It is said that he went out for a walk one day while his wife stayed at home alone. While he was away, an old woman came by begging. She looked like a Buddhist priestess <term>a female monk or nun, known as a biguni in the Korean tradition</term>, for although she was very old, her face was not wrinkled. The scholar’s wife asked her if she knew how to sew. She said she did, and so the wife made this offer: "If you will stay and work for me, I will give you breakfast and supper, and you will not have to beg anywhere. Will you agree?"

She replied, "Oh, thank you so much; I would be delighted."

The scholar’s wife, well satisfied with her bargain, took her in and set her to work picking cotton, making thread, and spinning. In one day, she did more than eight ordinary women, and yet she seemingly had plenty of time to spare. The wife, delighted beyond measure, treated her to a great feast. After five or six days, however, the feeling

<summary>A scholar's wife hires a mysterious old woman who resembles a Buddhist nun to help with sewing and spinning; the woman proves to be supernaturally efficient at her work.</summary>
<keywords>Confucian scholar, Seoul, Buddhist priestess, goblin, dokkaebi, spinning, cotton, Korean folklore</keywords>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the story "The Old Woman Who Became a Goblin," following the scholar's wife hiring a mysterious, highly efficient old woman who resembles a Buddhist nun.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>

<header>THE OLD WOMAN GOBLIN</header>

The initial feeling of delight and the desire to treat her generously and well wore off somewhat. Eventually, the old woman grew angry and said, “I am tired of living alone, so I want your husband to be my partner.” When this was refused, she left in a rage, but she returned a short time later accompanied by a worn-out old man who looked like a Buddhist beggar.

These two entered the room boldly and took possession of it. They cleared out the items in the ancient ancestral tablet-box <note>original: "tablet-box"; a container for the wooden tablets representing deceased ancestors, which were central to Confucian family rituals and kept on a high shelf.</note> on the wall shelf and both disappeared inside it. They were no longer seen at all, though their voices could still be heard. Depending on whatever whim struck them, they now demanded food and other things. Whenever the scholar’s wife failed in the slightest way to please them, they sent plague and sickness upon her, so that her children fell ill and died. When relatives heard of this and came to visit, they also caught the plague, became sick, and died. 

Gradually, no one dared to come near the house, and it finally became known that the wife was being held prisoner by these two goblin creatures. For a time, the townspeople saw smoke coming out of the chimney daily and knew that the wife was still alive; however, after five or six days, the smoke stopped. They knew then that the woman’s life had ended. No one even dared to go and investigate.

### XVIII

## THE GRATEFUL GHOST

It is often told that during the era of the Goryeo Dynasty <note>original: "Koryo Dynasty" (918–1392); the kingdom that ruled the Korean Peninsula and established many of the cultural and political systems used by the later Joseon Dynasty.</note>, when a national examination <note>original: "examination"; referring to the *gwageo*, the rigorous civil service exams required to become a government official.</note> was about to be held, a certain scholar traveled from a very distant part of the country to participate. At one point on his journey, as the day was ending, he found himself among the mountains. Suddenly, he heard the sound of sneezing coming from among the vines and bushes by the roadside, but he could see no one. Thinking this was strange, he dismounted from his horse, went into the thicket <note>original: "brake"; a dense growth of bushes or shrubs.</note>, and listened. He heard it again, and it seemed to come from the roots of a vine close beside him, so he ordered his servant to dig around it and see what was there. The servant dug and found a human skull. It was filled with earth, and the roots of the vine had grown through the nostrils. The sneezing was caused by the irritation the spirit felt because its nose was so obstructed <note>original: "discommoded"</note>.

The candidate felt pity for the soul. He washed the skull in clean water, wrapped it in paper, and reburied it in its original place on the hillside. He also brought a

<vocab>Goryeo Dynasty, civil service examination, scholar, spirit, skull</vocab>

<summary>A scholar traveling to take his civil service exams discovers a human skull tangled in vine roots; after realizing the spirit was sneezing in discomfort, he cleans and reburies it.</summary>
<keywords>Goryeo Dynasty, Gwagoe, ghosts, piety, scholar, supernatural, kindness to the dead</keywords>

table of food, offered a sacrifice, and said a prayer.

That night, in a dream, a scholar came to him—an old man with white hair who bowed, thanked him, and said, “Because of a sin committed in a former life <note>referring to the Buddhist and shamanic concept of karma, where actions in a past life dictate one’s current fate.</note>, I died prematurely <note>original: "out of season"</note> before I had lived out my natural days. My descendants <note>original: "posterity"</note>, too, were all destroyed, and my body crumbled back into the dust. Only my skull remained, and that is what you found beneath the vine. Because the root was passing through it, the irritation was great, and I could not help but sneeze. By good luck, you and your kind heart—which is blessed by Heaven <note>the divine moral order or "Chon" that governs fate in East Asian philosophy.</note>—took pity on me, buried me in a clean place, and gave me food. Your kindness is greater than the mountains and is like the blessing that first brought me into life. Although my soul is by no means perfect, I long for a way to repay <note>original: "requite"</note> your favor, and so I have used my powers on your behalf. Since your current journey is for the purpose of taking the official Civil Service Examination <note>original: "Examination"; referring to the *gwageo*, the prestigious national exams required to become a government official.</note>, I shall tell you beforehand what the format and the subject will be. It will consist of five-character lines in couplets <note>a classic form of poetry used in the exams, where each line contains five Chinese characters.</note>. The required rhyme sound is ‘pong’ <note>In these exams, candidates were given a specific rhyme category (the "un") that they had to use for the end of their verses.</note>, and the subject is ‘Peaks and Spires of the Summer Clouds.’ I have already composed a poem for you, which, if you care to use it, will undoubtedly win you the first place. It is this—”

> “The white sun rode high in the heavens,
> And the floating clouds formed a towering peak;
> The priest who saw them asked if a temple stood there,
> And the crane mourned <note>original: "lamented"</note> the fact that no pines were visible;
> But the lightning from the clouds was the flashing of the woodcutter’s axe,
> And the muffled thunder was the ringing bell of the holy temple.
> Will anyone say that the hills do not move?
> On the sunset breezes, they sailed away.”

After saying this, he bowed and took his leave.

Filled with wonder, the man woke from his dream and traveled up to Seoul; and look, the exam topic was exactly as the spirit had predicted. He wrote down the lines that had been given to him and won the highest honors of the occasion <note>meaning he took first place in the "gwageo," the national civil service examination.</note>.

### XIX

## THE PLUCKY MAIDEN

[**Han Myong-hoi.**—We are told in the *Yol-ryok Keui-sul* <note>original: *Yol-ryok Keui-sul*; an encyclopedic history of the Joseon Dynasty written by Yi Keung-ik</note> that when Han was a boy, he had a tiger for a protector and friend, who used to accompany him just as a dog follows his master. One evening, when he started off into the hills, he heard the distant heavy footsteps of the great beast, who had caught the scent of his departure and had come rushing after him. When Han saw him, he turned and said, “Good old fellow, you have come all this distance to be my friend; I love you for it.” The tiger prostrated himself <note>bowed low to the ground</note> and nodded his head several times. He used to accompany Han all through the nights, but when the day dawned, he would leave him.

Han later fell into bad company, grew fond of drink, and was one of the boisterous <note>rowdy or energetic</note> companions of King Se-jo.]

When Han served as governor of Pyong-an Province, he committed all kinds of lawless acts. Anyone who dared to oppose his wishes in the slightest was punished with torture and death. The entire province feared him as they would a tiger.

One day, Governor Han heard that the Deputy Prefect <note>a local government official ranking below a prefect</note> of Son-chon had a very beautiful daughter. Someone has given you a false report. But since you command it, how can I do anything but agree gladly?" He bowed, said his farewells, and went home.

### XX

## THE RESOURCEFUL WIFE

During the final year of King Yeonsangun's <note>original: "Yon-san"; Yeonsangun (reigned 1494–1506) is historically regarded as the most tyrannical king of the Joseon Dynasty.</note> reign, terrible evils plagued the people. Acts of wickedness unlike anything the world had ever seen were committed, with the King himself as the mastermind behind them. He even ordered his <term>eunuchs</term> <note>Castrated men who served as high-ranking palace officials and personal attendants to the King.</note> and subordinates to bring him any exceptionally beautiful women they saw in the homes of the highest nobility. Whoever pleased him, he took for himself. "Ignore any objections," he said. "Take them by force and bring them here." Such were his orders. No one was safe from him. He even went so far as to publicly claim that a certain Minister’s wife preferred him over her husband and wished to live in the Palace forever. This was the talk of the city, and the people were shocked.

For these reasons, the hearts of the people turned against him. Because of this, he was dethroned, and King Jungjong <note>original: "Choong-jong"; he reigned from 1506–1544 after his half-brother Yeonsangun was deposed in a coup.</note> reigned in his place.

During those troubled days, there lived a young wife of a certain minister <note>A high-ranking government official of the aristocratic "yangban" class.</note> who was remarkably beautiful in both form and face. One day, it happened that she was

<summary>This page describes the tyranny of King Yeonsangun, specifically his exploitation of noblewomen, which led to his dethronement and the rise of King Jungjong. It introduces a beautiful minister's wife who is caught up in these dangerous times.</summary>

<keywords>King Yeonsangun, King Jungjong, Joseon Dynasty, tyranny, Minister's wife, palace scandals, Korean folklore</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the story "The Resourceful Wife," picking up from the moment a beautiful minister’s wife is summoned to the palace of the tyrant King Yeonsangun.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>

# THE RESOURCEFUL WIFE

...ordered into the Palace. Other women, when summoned, would weep and behave as though their lives were lost, but this young woman showed not the slightest sign of fear. She dressed herself and went straight into the Palace. King Yeonsangun <note>original: "Yon-san"</note> saw her and ordered her to come close to him. She approached, and suddenly, the most terrible odor <note>original: "odour"</note> imaginable became noticeable. The King held his fan before his face, turned aside, spat, and said, "Good heavens, I cannot stand this one! Take her away!" And so, she escaped untouched.

This is how it happened: She knew she was likely to be called at any moment, and so she had planned a trick <note>original: "ruse"</note> by which to escape. She had kept two slices of meat constantly on hand that were decayed and foul-smelling. She placed these under her arms as she dressed to go into the Palace, providing that awful and mysterious stench.

All who knew of the incident praised her bravery and her wisdom <note>original: "sagacity"</note>.

# XXI

## THE BOXED-UP GOVERNOR

A certain scholar-official <note>original: "literary official"</note> was at one time Governor of the city of Gyeongju <note>original: "Kyong-ju"</note>. Whenever he visited the local Mayor, it was his habit, upon seeing the dancing-girls <note>These were *kisaeng*, professional female entertainers of the Joseon dynasty who were often highly educated in poetry and music.</note>, to tap them on the head with his pipe and say, “These girls are devils, ogres, and goblins. How can you tolerate them in your presence?”

Naturally, those who heard this disliked him, and the Mayor himself detested his behavior and manners. He sent a secret message to the dancing-girls, saying, “If any of you, by any means whatever, can deceive this governor and put him to shame, I will reward you richly.” Among them, there was one girl, a mere child, who said she could.

The Governor lived in the district of the city where the Confucian Temple was located, and he had only one servant with him, a young lad. The dancing-girl who had decided to trap <note>original: "ensnare"</note> him began to pass by the main gateway of the Temple frequently, dressed as an ordinary townswoman, and in

> "She is my sister," the boy said. "Her husband went away on a peddling trip <note>original: "peddling round"</note> a year or so ago and has not yet returned. Consequently, she has no one else to help her, so she frequently calls and talks <note>original: "confers"</note> with me."

One evening, when the boy had gone to eat his meal and the Governor was alone, the woman came to the main gateway and called for the boy.

The woman went and entered the house she had indicated. When evening came, she sent the hat with the boy. The Governor arrived as agreed, and she received him, lit the lamp, and brought him refreshments and drink. They talked and drank together, and then he called her to come to him.

She replied, “That is not yours; I bought that myself with two rolls of silk goods.”

“But,” said he, “I gave you one of those rolls, and I am not going to let you have it.”

“Even though you did give it, do you mean to say that for one roll of silk you will carry away this box? I will not consent to it.” Thus they quarreled and contradicted each other.

“If you do not give me the box,” he said, “I will file a lawsuit <note>original: "enter a suit"</note> against you at the Mayor’s office.”

A little later, day dawned, and so he had the box carried off to the Mayor’s office to have the case decided by law, while the woman followed. When they entered the court, the Mayor was already seated in the place of judgment, and there they presented their case concerning the box.

### XXII

## THE MAN WHO LOST HIS LEGS

There was a merchant in Cheongju <note>original: "Chong-ju"; a major city in central South Korea.</note> who used to go to Jeju Island <note>original: "Quelpart"; the historical European name for Korea's largest island, famous for its unique culture and sea products.</note> to buy seaweed. One time when he pulled up on the shore, he saw a man shuffling along the ground toward the boat. The man crept nearer, and finally took hold of the side of the boat with both hands and jumped in.

“When I looked at him,” the merchant said, “I found he was an old man without any legs. Astonished, I asked him, ‘Old man, how did you lose your legs?’

He replied, ‘I lost my legs once on a trip when I was shipwrecked, and a great fish bit them off.’

‘How did that ever happen?’ the merchant asked. 

The old man said, ‘We were caught in a gale and driven until we landed on some island or other. Before us on the shore stood a high castle with a great gateway. The twenty or so of us who were together in the storm-tossed boat were all exhausted from cold and hunger, and lying

<summary>A merchant visiting Jeju Island encounters a legless man who begins to tell a harrowing story of a shipwreck and a monstrous fish.</summary>

exposed. We landed and managed to go together to the house. There was in it one man only, whose height was terrible to behold, and whose chest was many spans <note>A span is an ancient measurement based on the width of a human hand, about 9 inches; "many spans round" indicates an immense chest.</note> round. His face was black and his eyes large and rolling. His voice was like the braying of a monster donkey. Our people gestured to show that they wanted something to eat. The man made no reply, but securely fastened the front gate. After this, he brought an armful of wood, put it in the middle of the courtyard, and there made a fire. When the fire blazed up, he rushed after us and caught a young lad, one of our company, cooked him before our eyes, pulled him to pieces, and ate him. We were all paralyzed with horror, not knowing what to do. We gazed at each other in dismay and shock <note>original: "stupefaction"</note>.

When he had eaten his fill, he went up into a veranda <note>original: "verandah"</note> and opened a jar, from which he drank some kind of alcohol <note>original: "spirit"</note>. # XXIII

## TEN THOUSAND DEVILS

[Han Chun-kyom was the son of a provincial secretary. He passed his preliminary civil service exams in 1579 and graduated with the highest honors in 1586. He was present to receive the final commands of King Seonjo <note>original: "Son-jo"; Seonjo was the fourteenth king of the Joseon Dynasty, reigning from 1567 to 1608.</note> and sat by the King's side for seven hours taking notes. From 1608 to 1623, he served as the commander-in-chief of the army and was later raised to the rank of Prince.]

A certain Prince Han of Chungcheong <note>original: "Choong-chong"</note> Province had a distant relative who was a rough countryman living in extreme poverty. This relative came to visit him from time to time. Han pitied his cold and hungry condition, gave him clothes to wear, and shared his food, often urging him to stay and prolong his visit for several months. While he felt sorry for the man, Han disliked his unrefined manners and lack of intelligence.

On one of these visits, the poor relative suddenly announced his intention to return home, even though the New Year’s season was just approaching. Han urged him to remain, saying, “It would be better—"

<summary>This page introduces the historical figure Han Chun-kyom and begins a tale about a wealthy prince who provides for a poor, unrefined relative. The story opens with the relative deciding to leave the prince's home just before the New Year.</summary>

He said at first that he would have to go, until his host urged him so insistently to stay that he finally yielded and gave his consent. On New Year’s Eve, he remarked to Prince Han, “I possess a peculiar kind of magic by which I control all manner of evil spirits <note>original: "genii"; in this context, referring to supernatural beings or demons</note>. New Year is the season when I call them up, review their names, and inspect them. If I did not do so, I would lose control over them altogether, and endless trouble for mortals would follow. It is a matter of great importance <note>original: "no small moment"</note>, and that is why I wished to leave. Since you have detained me, however, I will have to call them up in your Excellency’s house and look them over. I hope you will not object.”

Han was greatly astonished and alarmed but gave his consent. The poor relative went on to say, “This is an extremely important matter, and I would like to use your central guest hall <note>In a traditional Korean home, the *sarangchae* or guest hall was the social center for the men of the house</note> for it.”

Han consented to this as well, so that night they washed the floors and scoured them clean. The relative sat himself with great dignity facing the south, while Prince Han took his place on

### XXIV

## THE HOME OF THE FAIRIES <note>original: "Fairies"; In the context of Korean folklore, "fairies" usually refers to *sinseon*, which are Taoist immortals or transcendent mountain spirits, rather than the small, winged creatures of Western myth.</note>

In the days of King In-jo <note>In-jo (reigned 1623–1649) was the sixteenth king of the Joseon Dynasty.</note> there was a student of Confucius <note>original: "student of Confucius"; This refers to a scholar of the Joseon Dynasty's state ideology, Neo-Confucianism, who would have spent his life studying the Chinese classics.</note> who lived in Ka-pyong. He was still a young man and unmarried. His education had not been extensive, for he had read only a little in the way of history and literature. For some reason or other, he left his home and went into Kang-won Province <note>A mountainous province in eastern Korea, famous for the Diamond Mountains and its rugged terrain.</note>. Traveling on horseback with a servant, he reached a mountain where he was overtaken by rain that soaked him through.

Mysteriously, and for some unknown cause, his servant suddenly died. In fear and distress, the man dragged the body to the side of the hill, where he left it and went on his way weeping. When he had gone only a short distance, the horse he rode collapsed under him and died also. This was his dire situation: his servant was dead, his horse was dead, the rain was falling fast, and the road was unknown to him. He did not know what to do or where to go, and reduced thus to walking, he broke down and cried. At this point, he met an old man with very wonderful eyes and hair as white as snow. He asked the

<summary>A young Confucian scholar traveling through Kang-won Province is left stranded and weeping after his servant and horse both die mysteriously during a storm. In his despair, he encounters a remarkable old man with snow-white hair.</summary>

<keywords>King In-jo, Ka-pyong, Kang-won Province, Confucian scholar, Taoist immortal, mountain spirits, supernatural encounter</keywords>

...young man why he wept, and the reply was that his servant was dead, his horse was dead, that it was raining, and that he did not know the way. The old man <note>original: "patriarch"</note>, on hearing this, took pity on him, and lifting his staff, pointed, saying, “There is a house over there, just beyond those pines; follow that stream and it will bring you to where there are people.”

The young man looked where he was directed, and a *li* <term>li: a traditional unit of distance, roughly one-third of a mile or 400 meters</term> or so away, he saw a clump of trees. He bowed, thanked the stranger, and started on his way. When he had gone a few steps, he looked back, but the friend had disappeared. Wondering greatly, he went on toward the place pointed out, and as he drew near, he saw a grove of pines—huge trees, a whole forest of them. Bamboos appeared, too, in countless numbers, with a wide stream of water flowing by. Beneath the water, there appeared to be a marble floor like a great pavement, white and pure. As he went along, he saw that the water was all at a uniform depth, so that one could cross easily. A mile or so farther on, he saw a beautifully decorated house. The pillars and entrance ways were perfect in form. He continued his way, wet as he was, carrying his walking stick <note>original: "thorn staff"</note>, and entered the gate and sat down to rest. It was paved with marble as well, and was as smooth as...

In the room was a marble table, and on it sat a copy of the <term>Book of Changes</term> <note>original: "I Ching," an ancient Chinese divination text often studied by scholars and sages</note>. There was also a jade incense burner <note>original: "brazier"</note> just in front of it. Incense was burning inside, and the fragrance filled the room. Besides these items, nothing else was visible. The rain had stopped, and all was quiet and clear, with no wind or anything else to disturb the peace. The world of confusion <note>original: "world of confusion" refers to the chaotic, secular world of human affairs</note> seemed to have receded from him.

While he sat there, looking around in astonishment, he suddenly heard the sound of footsteps from the back of the building. Startled, he turned to look, and an old man appeared. He looked as though he might be as old as a turtle or a crane <note>In Korean and Chinese folklore, the turtle and crane are two of the "Ten Symbols of Longevity" and are said to live for centuries</note>, and he was very dignified. He wore a green robe <note>original: "dress"</note> and carried a jade staff made of nine sections. The old man’s appearance was so extraordinary that it would stun any inhabitant of the earth. The young man recognized him as the master of the place, so he went forward and made a low, respectful bow <note>original: "obeisance"</note>.

The old man received him kindly and said, “I am the master here, and I have long waited for you.” He took the young man by the hand and led him away. As they walked along, the hills grew more and more enchanting, while the soft breezes and the light touched him with a mysterious grace. Suddenly, as he—

When he looked back, the man was gone, so he continued on by himself. Soon, he arrived at another palace, also built from precious gemstones. It was a massive hall, stretching into the distance as far as the eye could see.

The young man had frequently seen the Royal Palace while in Seoul to take his civil service examinations <note>original: "examinations"; these were the competitive Gwageo exams used to recruit government officials in Joseon-era Korea</note>, but compared to this place, the Royal Palace looked like a simple mud hut thatched with straw.

As he reached the gate, a man in ceremonial robes greeted him and led him inside. He passed two or three pavilions and finally reached a special one, where he climbed to the upper floor. There, reclining at a table, he saw the ancient sage he had met before. Once again, the young man bowed.

Because this young man had been raised in poverty in the countryside, he was not accustomed to seeing or dealing with the Great <note>original: "the great" refers to high-ranking nobility or powerful officials</note>. In his fear, he did not dare to lift his eyes. The ancient master, however, welcomed him again and asked him to be seated, saying, “This is not the dusty world <note>original: "dusty world"; a common East Asian metaphor for the chaotic and material world of mortal humans</note> you are used to, but the home of the immortals <note>original: "genii"; in this context, the term refers to the *Shinseon*, or Daoist immortals, rather than the "genies" of Middle Eastern folklore</note>. I knew you were coming, so I was waiting to receive you.” He turned and called out, “Bring something for the guest to eat.”

In a short time, a servant brought a table laden with rich food. It was a feast unlike anything ever seen on earth, and...

Two boys, about twelve or thirteen years of age, came running in and sat down beside him. Their faces were so beautifully white they seemed like jewels. The two boys counted the days on their fingers and then said together, "The day after tomorrow is a lucky day."

The old man turned to the stranger and said, "That settles the wedding date. Now, you must wait in the guest room <note>original: "guest-chamber"</note> until the time arrives." He then gave a command to call for a certain person <note>original: "So and So"</note>. Soon, an...

An official of the immortals <note>original: "genii"; here referring to Taoist spirits or supernatural beings who inhabit the celestial realms</note> came forward, dressed in light and airy garments. His appearance and expression were very beautiful; he seemed to be a man of a glad and happy manner <note>original: "mien"; a person’s look or appearance, especially as it indicates their character or mood</note>.

The master said, “Show this young man the way to his apartments and treat him well until the time of the wedding.”

The official then led the way, and the young man bowed as he left the room. When he had passed outside the gate, a red sedan chair <note>original: "sedan chair"; an enclosed chair carried on poles by bearers, used as a primary mode of transportation for the elite</note> was waiting for him. He was asked to get inside. Eight bearers carried him smoothly along. About a mile away, they reached another palace, equally wonderful, with no speck or flaw of any kind to spoil its beauty. Amid graceful groves of flowers and trees, he stepped out to enter his pavilion. Beautiful garments were taken from jeweled boxes; he was given a perfumed bath and a change of clothes. Thus, he laid aside his weather-beaten clothes and put on <note>original: "donned"</note> the robes <note>original: "vestments"</note> of the immortals. The official remained as his companion until the appointed time.

Her face was like a freshly-blown lotus flower. Here, a great feast was spread with many invited guests. The musical accompaniment was more beautiful than anything mortals ever dreamed of. When the feast was over, the women gathered up their skirts and, lifting their sleeves, danced together and sang in sweet harmony. The sound of their singing caused even the clouds to stop and listen. When the day was over and everyone had dined well, the feast broke up.

A young man, raised in a humble country hut, had suddenly met the leader of the spirits <note>original: "genii"; in Korean folklore, these are the *Sinseon*, or Taoist immortals who live in the mountains and possess magical powers.</note> and had come to share in his glory and his way of life. His mind was dazed, and his thoughts overwhelmed him. Doubts were mixed with fears, and he did not know what to do.

He had truly become a participant in the joys of the fairies, and about a year passed in such delight that no words can ever describe it.

One day his wife said to him, "Would you like to enter the inner garden <note>original: "inner enclosure"; likely referring to a more sacred or private spiritual realm within the palace grounds.</note> and see as the fairies see?"

The scene grew gradually more enchanting, with jewelled flowers and sparkling spray, where lovely birds and animals played about. Anyone who entered here once would never think of Earth as a place to return to again.

Before him lay a vast stretch of sea, with the Islands of the Blessed <note>original: "islands of the blessed"; in Taoist mythology, these are the legendary islands like Penglai where immortals live and the elixir of life is found.</note> rising out of the water, and long stretches of pleasant land in sight. They were inhabited by happy souls, some riding on cranes, some on the phoenix, some on the unicorn <note>original: "unicorn"; likely the *Girin* (or *Qilin*), a mythical hooved creature with a single horn that symbolizes prosperity and serenity.</note>; some were sitting on the clouds, some sailing by on the wind, some walking on the air, some gliding gently up the streams, some descending from above, some ascending, some moving west, some north, and some gathering in groups. The young man had a close friend whom he had known intimately since childhood. After his return, this friend visited him frequently, and they often spent the nights talking together. When spring came, the spring clothes were sufficient; when summer came, those for summer; and for autumn and winter, there was a special suit for each. They were never washed, yet never became soiled; they never wore out and always looked fresh and new. The friend was greatly astonished.

Some three years passed when, one day, a servant from the master of the immortals arrived once more, bringing the man’s two sons. There were also letters, saying:

> “Next year, the place where you dwell will be destroyed and all the people will become ‘fish and meat’ <note>A traditional idiom meaning to be slaughtered or treated as helpless victims of an invading army.</note> for the enemy; therefore, follow this messenger and come, all of you.”

He told his friend of this and showed him his two sons. When the friend saw these children, who looked like “silk and jade” <note>A classical idiom used to describe children of extraordinary beauty, health, and refinement.</note>, he confessed the matter to the mother as well. She, too, gladly agreed to go. And so they sold their property and held a great feast for all the people of the town, and then bade them farewell. This was the year 1635 <note>Historically significant, as the Manchu Invasion of 1636 (the *Byeongja Horan*) occurred the following year, fulfilling the prophecy of destruction.</note>. They left and were never heard of again.

<header>XXV</header>

[Song Sang-in passed his preliminary civil service examinations <note>original: "matriculated"; in the Joseon Dynasty context, this refers to passing the *Sogwa* exams</note> in 1601. He was a just man, feared by the corrupt members of the royal court. In 1605, he passed the highest level of the exams and became a provincial governor. He nearly lost his life during the political turmoil of King Gwanghaegun’s <note>original: "King Kwang-hai"; Gwanghaegun (r. 1608–1623) was the 15th king of the Joseon Dynasty, eventually deposed in a coup</note> reign and was exiled to Jeju Island <note>original: "Quelpart"; a name used by early European navigators for the island of Jeju</note> for ten years, but in the spring of 1623, he was recalled to office.]

## THE HONEST SHAMAN <note>original: "WITCH"; the author is referring to the *mudang*, the traditional practitioners of Korean shamanism</note>

### *The Story*

There was once a Korean man named Song Sang-in, whose mind was upright and whose spirit was sincere. He hated shamans with all his might and regarded them as people who deceived the public.

“Through their so-called prayers,” he said, “they consume the people’s wealth. There is no limit to the foolishness and waste that follows them. Their doctrine is complete nonsense. If only I could rid the earth of them and wipe out their names forever.”

<summary>This page introduces Song Sang-in, a principled official of the Joseon Dynasty who was exiled to Jeju Island and held a deep-seated hatred for shamans, whom he viewed as social parasites.</summary>

<keywords>Song Sang-in, King Gwanghaegun, Jeju Island, Quelpart, shamanism, mudang, Joseon Dynasty history</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Song Sang-in, focusing on his tenure as magistrate of Namwon and his attempt to eliminate shamanism from the district.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

Some time later, Song was appointed magistrate of Namwon <note>original: "Nam Won"</note> County in Jeolla <note>original: "Chulla"</note> Province. Upon his arrival, he issued the following order: “If any shaman <note>original: "witch"</note> is found in this county, let her be beaten to death.” The entire area was so thoroughly watched by spies that all the shamans fled to other districts. The magistrate thought, “Now we are rid of them, and that settles the matter for this county, at least.”

One day, he went out for a walk and rested for a while at the *Gwanghanru* <note>original: "Kwang-han"; a famous pavilion in Namwon, celebrated in Korean literature as the meeting place of the lovers Chunhyang and Mongryong</note> Pavilion. As he looked out from his vantage point, he saw a woman approaching on horseback with a shaman’s drum on her head. He looked closely to be sure, and to his astonishment, he saw that she was indeed a *mudang* <term>shaman</term> <note>original: "mutang (witch)"</note>. He sent a government runner <term>yamen-runner</term> to have her arrested, and when she was brought before him, he asked, “Are you a shaman?”

She replied, “Yes, I am.”

“Then,” he said, “did you not know about the official order I issued?”

“Oh yes, I heard of it,” she replied.

He then asked, “Are you not afraid to die, staying here in this county?”

The shaman bowed and answered, “I have a grievance to bring before Your Excellency to be corrected; please listen and grant my request. It is this: there are true shamans...”

<summary>Upon becoming magistrate of Namwon, Song Sang-in bans all shamans on pain of death, but he is eventually confronted by a defiant mudang at the Gwanghanru Pavilion who wishes to argue her case.</summary>

<keywords>Namwon, Jeolla Province, Gwanghanru Pavilion, mudang, shamanism, Song Sang-in, yamen-runner</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the magistrate Song Sang-in and a shaman (mudang) who has challenged his decree to execute all shamans in the district.</meta>

<header>THE HONEST SHAMAN <note>original: "WITCH"</note></header>

"...and false *mudangs* <term>shamans</term> <note>original: "mutangs"</note>. False *mudangs* ought to be killed, but you would not kill an honest *mudang*, would you? Your orders apply <note>original: "pertain"</note> to false *mudangs*; I do not understand them as applying to those who are true. I am an honest *mudang*; I knew you would not kill me, so I remained here in peace."

The magistrate asked, "How do you know that there are honest *mudangs*?"

The woman replied, "Let us put the matter to the test and see. If I am not proven honest, then let me die."

"Very well," said the magistrate; "but can you really prove your claim <note>original: "make good"</note>, and do you truly know how to call back the spirits of the dead?"

The *mudang* answered, "I can."

The magistrate suddenly thought of a close <note>original: "intimate"</note> friend who had been dead for some time, and he said to her, "I had a friend of a certain rank in Seoul; can you call his spirit back to me?"

The *mudang* replied, "Allow me to do so; but first you must prepare food, with wine, and serve it properly."

The magistrate thought for a moment, and then said to himself, "It is a serious matter to take a person's life; let me find out first if she is genuine or not, and then decide." So he ordered the food to be brought.

She then lifted her eyes toward heaven and chanted <note>original: "uttered"</note> the strange magic sounds by which spirits are called, while shaking a tinkling bell. After a short while, she turned and said, “I’ve come.” Then she began telling the sad story of the friend's sickness and death, and of their separation. She reminded the magistrate of how they had played together as children, and of things that had happened when they were at school during their lessons; of the difficulties they had faced in the civil service examinations <note>The Gwagyeo, or national exams for government positions</note>; and of experiences they had shared during their terms of office. She told secrets that they had confided to each other as intimate friends, and many specific matters that only the two of them knew. She did not make a single mistake, but told the truth in every detail.

The magistrate, when he heard these things, began to cry, saying, “The soul of my friend is really present; I can no longer doubt or deny it.” Then he ordered the finest food <note>original: "choicest fare"</note> possible to be prepared as a sacrifice <note>A ritual offering to the spirit of the deceased</note> to his friend. After a short time, the friend bade him farewell and took his departure.

## XXVI

# WHOM THE KING HONORS

In the days of King Se-jong <note>King Se-jong the Great (reigned 1418–1450) is one of Korea’s most celebrated rulers, famous for creating the Korean alphabet.</note>, students of the Confucian College <note>The Sungkyunkwan, the highest educational institution during the Joseon Dynasty.</note> were having a picnic to celebrate the Spring Festival. They met in a forest to the north of the college, near a beautiful spring, and were drinking and feasting all through the night. While they were enjoying themselves this way, the rooms of the college were left deserted. One student from the countryside—something of a "country bumpkin" <note>original: "backwoodsman"</note> who was considered unimportant by the others—thought that while the rest went away to enjoy themselves, someone ought to stay behind to guard the sacred grounds <note>original: "precincts"</note> of the temple. So, he decided that he would give up the pleasures of the picnic, stay behind, and keep watch.

The King at that time sent a eunuch <term>eunuch</term> <note>a castrated male official who served the royal court</note> to the college to see how many of the students had remained on guard. The eunuch returned, saying that everyone had gone off to the picnic except for one man, an unsophisticated <note>original: "raw"</note> countryman, who was in sole charge. The King immediately sent for the man, asking him to come exactly as he was in his everyday clothes.

<vocab>King Se-jong, Confucian College, Spring Festival, eunuch, person from the country <note>original: "backwoodsman"</note>, student</vocab>

<summary>A humble country student decides to stay behind and guard the Confucian College while his peers go out to party, catching the attention of King Se-jong.</summary>

The King was greatly pleased with this reply and asked again, “Do you know how to write poetry?”

The student replied, “I know only a very little about it.”

While the students at the picnic were thus occupied, they suddenly heard the sound of flutes and harps, and—

## XXVII

# THE FORTUNES OF YOO

There was a man from Yongnam <note>the southeastern region of Korea, modern-day Gyeongsang province</note> named Yoo who lived during the reign of King Sejong <note>ruled 1418–1450</note>. He had studied the Confucian classics, passed the civil service examinations, and become a minor official attached to the Confucian College <note>the Sungkyunkwan, the kingdom's highest educational institution</note>. He had not even reached the sixth rank <note>original: "sixth degree." Joseon officials were categorized into nine ranks; reaching the sixth rank was a major milestone for career advancement</note>, so further promotion seemed impossible. He was a man from the country with no friends or influential connections, and though he had lived in Seoul for a long time, there was no prospect of him moving up in the world. Because of this, feeling discouraged and lonely, he decided to leave the capital and return to his home in the country.

There was a royal secretary <note>an official of the Royal Secretariat, or Seungjeongwon</note> who was acquainted with this man and went to say goodbye to him before he departed.

Taking advantage of the visit, Yoo said, "I have been in Seoul for a long time, but I have never yet seen the King’s Secretariat. Could I go with you some day when you are on duty <note>original: "take your turn"</note>?"

The secretary replied, "During the day, there is always a crowd of people who gather there for—"

Normally, people are not permitted to sleep in the Palace, but doing so just once won't be particularly noticed.” The secretary then ordered the military guard who accompanied him to escort Yoo inside the next day.

Following the secretary’s arrangement, the countryman entered the Palace grounds the next evening. To his surprise, however, he found that for some reason or other, the secretary had not arrived. Furthermore, the gates were closed behind him, so he could not get out. He was truly in a difficult situation. A personal servant <note>original: "body-servant"</note> of the secretary happened to be in the room; feeling sorry for the stranger, he arranged a hidden corner where Yoo could spend the night and then leave quietly in the morning.

The night was beautifully clear, and everyone except Yoo seemed to be asleep. He remained wide awake, wondering to himself if he might slip out quietly and see the place.

It was the rainy season <note>the *jangma* season, or summer monsoon, which often caused the traditional stone-and-earth walls of Seoul to weaken and collapse during heavy downpours</note>, and a section of the wall had fallen from the enclosure just in front. Yoo climbed over this broken wall and, not knowing where he was going, suddenly found himself—

When they met, Yoo had no idea that it was the King. His Majesty asked, "Who are you, and how did you get in here?"

Yoo explained who he was and how he had agreed to come in with the secretary. He told how the secretary had failed to arrive; how the gates were shut, making him a prisoner for the night; and how he had seen the bright moonlight and wished to walk out, and, finding the broken wall, had come over. "Whose house is this, anyway?" asked Yoo.

The King replied, "I am the master of this house." His Majesty then invited him in and had him sit down on a mat beside him. So they talked and chatted together. The King learned that Yoo had passed the special examinations <note>the *gwageo*, or state civil service examinations, which were the primary route to prestige and power in Joseon Korea</note> in the classics. When inquiring why it was that Yoo held no significant office, Yoo replied that he was an unknown countryman, that his family had no influence, and that, while he desired an official position, he was always blocked by the powerful families of the capital. The King was delighted and greatly amazed, and so they read together throughout the night. When they parted, the King sighed and said, "You possess all this knowledge and yet your talents have never been put to use? Alas for my country!"

Yoo remarked that he would like to go straight home now, if the master of the house would be kind enough to open the door for him.

The King said, however, that it was still too early, and that he might be arrested by the guards if he left now.

The next day, he appointed him to an office yet another degree higher. Again they all protested, and he seemingly yielded to them. But the following day, Yoo was promoted even higher still, and again the protests poured in—so much so that His Majesty appeared to yield. On the day after that, the King wrote out an appointment for him to the office of Vice-President of all the Literati <note>The *Literati* refers to the class of educated Confucian scholars who served as government officials in the Joseon Dynasty.</note>.

From 그hat time on, Yoo became the royal teacher of the *Choo-yuk* <note>original: *Ju-yeok*, the Korean pronunciation of the *I Ching* or *Book of Changes*, a foundational text of Confucian philosophy</note>, and rose higher and higher in rank until he became the Head of the Confucian College <note>The *Sungkyunkwan*, the supreme educational institution in Joseon-era Korea</note> and the most influential person in the kingdom, surpassing everyone else.

*Note.*—Many people of ability never receive a chance for promotion. It is difficult to have one’s gifts recognized by those in high places; how much more difficult is it to be noticed by a king? The good fortune that fell to the first scholar was by God’s appointment. By simply looking after a vacant house, honor came to him and he was promoted. The other man’s entry into the Palace uninvited was a great offense, but by royal favor he was pardoned, received, and honored.

By a single line of poetry, one man’s ability was revealed, and by a single explanation of the *Choo-yuk*, another man’s path was opened to high promotion.

### XXVIII

## AN ENCOUNTER WITH A HOBGOBLIN

I found myself in trouble in the year 1716 <note>original: Pyong-sin</note> and was imprisoned. A military officer named Choi Won-so, who was a captain of the guard, was also involved in the matter and was locked up as well. We often met in prison and passed the hours talking together. On a certain day, the conversation turned toward goblins <note>original: hobgoblins; in Korean folklore, these are often *tokkaebi*, mischievous spirits or imps known for playing tricks on humans</note>, and Captain Choi said, "When I was young, I encountered a hobgoblin, which, by a mere hair’s breadth, almost cost me my life. It was a strange case indeed!"

I asked him to tell me the story, and he replied, "I did not originally have a home in Seoul, but hearing of a vacant place in Belt Town <note>likely referring to the Tae-pyong-dong district of Seoul</note>, I applied for it and got it. We moved there; my father and the rest of the family occupied the inner rooms <note>the *anchae*, or the traditional women's and family quarters of a Korean house</note>, while I lived in the front room.

"One night, late, when I was half asleep, the door suddenly opened, and a woman came in and stood directly in front of the lamp. I saw her clearly and recognized her as someone from the household of a scholar friend of mine. I had seen her before and had been greatly attracted to her..."

<vocab>Pyong-sin, Choi Won-so, Seoul, Belt Town, hobgoblin</vocab>

We reached the gate, which she opened with a backward kick, and I followed after her until she suddenly disappeared. I searched everywhere, but there was not a trace of her. I thought she had simply hidden herself and never dreamed it could be anything else.

"On the next night, she came again and stood before the lamp just as she had done the night before. I was once more surprised and disappointed, but I still did not think of her being a hobgoblin <note>original: hobgoblin; in Korean folklore, these are often *tokkaebi*, spirits known for their supernatural tricks and ability to change form</note>.

"A few days later, at night, I had lain down when suddenly there was a sound of crackling paper from above the ceiling. It was a forbidding, creepy sound to hear at midnight. A moment later, a curtain was lowered that divided the room into two parts. Then, later still, a large fire of coals descended right in front of me, while an intense heat filled the place. *Note.*—There are various reasons why a place may be designated a “haunted house.” The fact that there are hobgoblins in it makes it haunted. If a good or “superior man” <note>original: "superior man"; a translation of the Confucian term *Junzi*, referring to a person of high moral character and integrity</note> enters such a place, the goblins move away, and no word of it being haunted will be heard. Choi saw the goblin and was seriously injured.

I understand that it is not only a matter of men fearing the goblins, but that they also fear men. The fact that there are so few people whom they fear is the saddest case of all. Choi was afraid of the goblins; that is why they troubled him.

->IM BANG.<-

### XXIX

## THE SNAKE’S REVENGE

There lived in ancient days an archer whose home was near the Water Gate <note>original: "Water Gate"; likely the *Sugumun* (also known as *Gwanghuimun*), an opening in Seoul’s city wall used for both water drainage and the passage of corpses out of the city</note> of Seoul. He was a man of great strength and famous for his valor.

The Water Gate refers to an opening under the city wall through which the waters of the Grand Canal <note>original: "Grand Canal"; this refers to the *Cheonggyecheon*, the historic stream that runs through the center of Seoul</note> flow out of the city. Iron pickets are installed there to prevent people from entering or leaving by that route.

On a certain afternoon, when this military officer was taking a walk, a great snake was seen making its way through the Water Gate. The snake’s head had already passed between the bars, but since its body was larger, it could not get through; thus, it was held fast. The soldier drew an arrow and, fitting it to the string, shot the snake in the head. Its head being fatally injured, the creature died. The archer then pulled it out, pounded it into a pulp, and left it.

A short time later, the man’s wife conceived and gave birth to a son. From the very beginning, the child was afraid of his father, and when he saw him, he used to cry and seem greatly frightened. As he grew, he hated...

<summary>An archer in Seoul kills a large snake trapped in the city’s water gate, only to find that his newborn son is mysteriously terrified of him.</summary>
<keywords>archer, Seoul, Water Gate, Sugumun, Grand Canal, Cheonggyecheon, snake, revenge, reincarnation, folklore</keywords>

<page-num>147</page-num>

<header>THE SNAKE'S REVENGE</header>

...the sight of his father more and more. The man became suspicious of this, and so, instead of loving his son, he grew to dislike him.

On a certain day, when there were just the two of them in the room, the officer lay down to take a midday nap <note>original: "siesta"</note>, covering his face with his sleeve, but all the while keeping his eye on the boy to see what he would do. The child glared at his father and, thinking him asleep, grabbed a knife and tried to stab him. The man jumped up, grabbed the knife, and then struck the boy with a club, killing him on the spot. He beat the body into a pulp <note>This brutal treatment mirrors how the archer originally killed the snake at the Water Gate.</note>, left him there, and went away. The mother, however, in tears, covered the small body with a quilt and prepared for its burial.

In a little while, the quilt began to move. In alarm, she raised it to see what had happened, when behold! Beneath it, the child was gone, and there lay a huge, coiled snake instead. The mother jumped back in fear, fled the room, and did not enter it again.

When evening came, the husband returned and heard the dreadful story from his wife. He went in and looked, and now the child had completely transformed <note>original: "metamorphosed"</note> into a huge snake. On its head was the scar from the arrow he had shot. He said to the snake, "You and I were not originally enemies; therefore, I did wrong in shooting you as I did. But your intention to take revenge through

<summary>The archer kills his own son in self-defense, only to witness the boy’s body transform back into the very snake he had killed years before, confirming the creature's supernatural vendetta.</summary>

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

"...becoming my son was a horrible deed. Such a thing as this is proof that my suspicions of you were right and just. You became my son in order to kill me, your father; why, therefore, should I not in my turn kill you? If you attempt it again, it will certainly end in my taking your life. You have already had your revenge and have once more returned to your original shape <note>original: "transmigrated into your original shape"</note>; let us drop the past and be friends from now on. What do you say?"

He repeated this many times and urged his proposals, while the snake, with its head bowed, seemed to listen intently. He then opened the door and said, "Now you may go as you please." The snake then departed, making straight for the Water Gate <note>A gate in a city wall designed to allow water to flow through. In old Seoul, these were often associated with the passage of spirits.</note>, and passed out between the bars. It never appeared again.

**Note.**—Man is a spiritual being and different from all other created things; although a snake has the power of venom, it is still an insignificant creature compared with a man. The snake died, and by means of the rebirth <note>original: "transmigration"</note> of its soul, it took its revenge. When a man dies, however, I have never heard that he can return in another form <note>original: "transmigrate"</note> as the snake did. Why is it that, although he is a spiritual being, he is unable to do what beasts do? I have seen many innocent men killed, but not one of them has ever returned to... <note>The text ends mid-sentence.</note>

<summary>The archer offers a truce to the snake, which accepts and departs through the city gate, leading the author to question why animals seem more capable of supernatural revenge than humans.</summary>

# XXX THE BRAVE MAGISTRATE

In the past, in one of the counties of North Ham-kyong Province, there was a foul-smelling goblin that caused great loss of life. One magistrate after another arrived, but within ten days or so of their arrival, each died in great agony. Because of this, no one wanted to take the post <note>original: "billet" — a position or assignment, often used in a military context.</note> or have anything to do with the place. A hundred or more people were asked to take the position, but they all refused. 

Finally, one brave soldier, who lacked any social or political influence, accepted. He was a courageous man, strong and fearless. He thought, "Even if there is a devil there, surely not everyone will die. I will test my luck." So he said his farewells and entered his office. He found himself alone in the <term>yamen</term> <note>original: "yamen" — the administrative office or residence of a local government official in East Asia.</note>, as everyone else had fled. He constantly carried a long knife at his belt and stayed armed, for he noticed from his first day a fishy, stinking odor that gradually became stronger and stronger.

After five or six days, he also noticed that what

It had no head, and neither a body nor arms were visible. Only at the top were two dreadful eyes, rolling wildly. 

The magistrate jumped up at once, rushed toward it with a great shout, and struck it with his sword. When he struck the blow, there was a sound like thunder, and the entire thing dissipated. The foul smell that accompanied it also vanished instantly.

The magistrate then, in his drunken state, fell face-down on the ground. The retainers, all thinking he was dead, gathered in the courtyard to prepare for his burial. They saw him lying on the ground, but they noted that the bodies of others who had died from this evil had always been found on the veranda <note>original: "verandah" — an open, roofed porch or platform on the side of a building.</note>, while his was in the lower courtyard. They lifted him up in order to...

### XXXI

## THE TEMPLE TO THE GOD OF WAR

[**Yi Hang-bok.** <note>Yi Hang-bok (1556–1618), also known by his pen name Baeksa, was a celebrated scholar-official who served as Chief State Councilor (Prime Minister) during the Joseon Dynasty. He is a beloved figure in Korean history, famous for his wit and his service during the Japanese invasions (1592–1598).</note>—When he was a child, a blind fortune-teller came and predicted his future, saying, "This boy will be very great indeed."

At seven years of age, his father gave him a subject to write a verse on: "The Harp and the Sword." He wrote—

> "The Sword belongs to the hand of the warrior,
>   And the Harp to the music of the ancients."

At eight, he took the subject of the "Willow Before the Door" and wrote—

> "The east wind brushes the brow of the cliff,
>   And the willow on the edge nods fresh and green."

Upon seeing a picture of a grand banquet held by the fierce Turks <note>In the context of this era, "Turks" refers generally to the nomadic peoples of Central Asia.</note> of Central Asia, he wrote this—

> "The hunt is over in the wild, dark hills,
>   And the moon is cold and gray,
> While the stomping feet of a thousand horses
>   Ring on the frosty way.
> In the tents of the Turks, the music thrills,
>   And the wine cups clink for joy,
> Amid the noise of the dancers' wild steps
>   And the lilt of the wild oboe <term>original: "hautboy," an archaic term for a double-reed woodwind instrument, the ancestor of the modern oboe</term>."

<summary>This page introduces the child prodigy Yi Hang-bok, showcasing his early poetic talent and a prophecy regarding his future greatness as a statesman.</summary>
<keywords>Yi Hang-bok, Joseon Dynasty, child prodigy, Korean poetry, fortune-telling, Central Asia, hautboy</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Yi Hang-bok, focusing on his transition from a spirited, generous youth to a distinguished scholar and vital statesman during the Japanese invasions (Imjin War).</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

At twelve years of age, he was described as proud and haughty. He dressed well and was envied by the poorer boys of the town. On one occasion, he took off his coat and gave it to a boy who looked at him with envy. He gave away his shoes as well and returned home barefoot. His mother, wanting to test his character, pretended to scold him, but he replied, "Mother, when others wanted them so much, how could I refuse to give them?" His mother pondered these things in her heart.

By the time he was fifteen, he was strong and well-built. He enjoyed vigorous exercise and became a well-known wrestler and was skillful at shuttlecock <term>shuttlecock: Likely a reference to *Jegichagi*, a traditional Korean game similar to hacky-sack where players kick a weighted feathered object to keep it in the air</term>. His mother, however, disapproved of these activities, saying they lacked dignity for a person of his status. Consequently, he gave them up and focused his attention on literary studies, passing his examinations <note>original: "graduating"; in this context, it refers to passing the *Gwageo*, the highest civil service examinations in the Joseon Dynasty</note> at twenty-five years of age.

In 1592, during the Japanese War <note>The Imjin War (1592–1598), when Japan, under Toyotomi Hideyoshi, invaded the Korean peninsula</note>, when the King <note>King Seonjo (reigned 1567–1608)</note> fled north to Uiju, Yi Hang-bok accompanied him. There he met a representative from the Ming Dynasty of China, who said in surprise to His Majesty, "Do you mean to tell me that you have men in Joseon <note>original: "Cho-sen," the name of the Korean kingdom during this period</note> as talented as Yi Hang-bok?" Yang Hao <note>A Ming general who led the Chinese forces sent to assist Korea</note>, the general of the rescuing forces, also continually sought his advice and counsel. He lived to see the political turmoil during the reign of King Gwanghaegun <note>original: "the wicked Kwang-hai." Gwanghaegun (reigned 1608–1623) was eventually deposed and has historically been depicted as a tyrant, though modern historians often view his foreign policy more favorably.</note>, and was eventually sent into exile in Buk- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; he was exiled to Bukcheong, a town in the northern part of Korea.</note>

<vocab>Yi Hang-bok, Japanese War, Uiju (Eui-ju), Ming Dynasty, Joseon (Cho-sen), Yang Hao (Yang Ho), Gwanghaegun (Kwang-hai), shuttlecock</vocab>

<summary>This page details Yi Hang-bok's youthful generosity and his later rise to prominence as a vital diplomatic and military advisor during the Imjin War.</summary>
<keywords>Yi Hang-bok, Imjin War, King Seonjo, Joseon Dynasty, Ming Dynasty, Gwanghaegun, Korean history, Jegichagi</keywords>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the statesman Yi Hang-bok. The previous page discussed his youth, his scholarly achievements, and his service to King Seonjo during the Japanese invasions (Imjin War).</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>

<header>THE TEMPLE TO THE GOD OF WAR</header>

...-hyeong <note>The text likely refers to Lee Deok-hyeong, Yi Hang-bok’s lifelong friend and fellow statesman; the two are famously known in Korean folklore as 'O-seong and Han-eum'</note>. When he crossed the Iron Pass <note>Cheollyeong Pass, a famous mountain pass near Wonsan often mentioned in Korean classical poetry</note> near Wonsan, he wrote—

> “From the dizzying height of the Iron Peak,
> I call out to the passing cloud:
> Take up a lonely exile’s tears
> In the folds of your feathery mist,
> And let them fall as rain on the Palace Gates,
> Upon the King and his shameless court.” <note>original: "shameless crowd"; Yi Hang-bok was sent into exile late in life due to court factionalism, and this poem expresses his enduring loyalty to King Gwanghaegun despite his treatment</note>]

->*The Story*<-

During the Japanese War <note>The Imjin War (1592–1598), a series of invasions by Japan under Toyotomi Hideyoshi</note> in the reign of King Seonjo, the Ming Dynasty <note>original: "the Mings"</note> sent a great army that came east, drove out the enemy, and restored peace. At that time, the general of the Ming forces informed the Korean King that their victory was due to the supernatural help of Guan Yu <note>original: "Kwan," the Chinese general of the Han Dynasty who was deified as the God of War</note>. “Since this is the case,” the general said, “you should not continue without temples in which to express your gratitude to him.” 

So, they built houses of worship for him and offered sacrifices. The temples built were located to the south and to the east of the city. While examining potential sites for these, the officials could not agree on the location for the South Temple. Some wanted it closer to the city wall, while others wanted it farther away. At that time, an official named Yi Hang-bok was in charge of the planning conference. One day, while Yi was at home, a military officer called and asked to see him. When Yi ordered him to enter, he found the man to be a massive, powerfully built fellow. The officer’s request was that Yi should...

...asked him to send out all his attendants <note>original: "retainers"</note> so that he could speak to him privately. They were sent out, and then the stranger delivered his message. When he had finished, he said goodbye and departed.

At that time, Yi <note>Yi Hang-bok (1556–1618), a famous Joseon official known for his wit and loyalty</note> had an old friend staying with him. The friend had gone out with the servants when they were asked to leave, and he now returned. When he came in, he noticed that the master’s face bore a very strange expression, and he asked him the reason for it. Yi did not answer at first, but later he told his friend that a very extraordinary thing had happened. 

The soldier who had come to visit was none other than a messenger from the God of War <note>Guan Yu, a deified Chinese general</note>. His visit was because the officials had not yet decided on the site for the Temple. “He came,” said Yi, “to show me where it ought to be. He insisted that it was not a matter for the present time only, but for all the ages to come. If we do not get it right, the God of War will find no peace. I told him in reply that I would do my best. Was this not strange?”

The friend who heard this was deeply troubled, but Yi warned him not to repeat the story to anyone. Yi exerted all his influence, and at last, the building was placed on the approved site, where it stands today.

## A VISIT FROM THE SHADES

[CHOI YU-WON.—(The story of meeting his mother’s ghost is reported to concern this man.)
Choi Yu-won passed his preliminary exams <note>original: "matriculated"</note> in 1579 and passed the highest civil service exam <note>original: "graduated"</note> in 1602. He eventually became Chief Justice and was granted the rank of prince <note>In the Joseon Dynasty, the title of "Prince" (Gun) was often conferred upon high-ranking officials as an honorary distinction, even if they were not members of the royal family</note>. When he was a boy, his great-aunt once gave him fabric for a suit of clothes, but he refused to accept it. From this, his aunt predicted that he would one day become a famous man. He studied in the home of the great teacher Yul-gok <note>Yi I (1536–1584), one of Korea’s two greatest Neo-Confucian scholars</note>, and Yul-gok also predicted that the day would come when he would be an honor to Korea.

Yu-won once met Chang Han-kang <note>Chang Hyon-kwang (1554–1637), a renowned philosopher and scholar-official</note> and asked him about *<term>Pyon-hwa Gijil</term>* <note>original: "Pyon-wha Keui-jil"</note> (the transformation of one’s natural temperament—a philosophical principle by which the weak become strong, the wicked become good, and the ignorant become wise). He also asked whether, if a person is truly transformed, the soul changes as well as the body, or the body only? Chang replied, “Both are changed, for how could the body change without the soul?” Yu-won asked Yul-gok con—

In 1607, Choi Yu-won submitted a formal petition <note>original: "memorialized"</note> to the King, calling attention to a letter received from Japan. This was an answer to a communication sent by His Majesty, but the address on the Japanese letter listed the name of the Prime Minister one space lower than proper etiquette required <note>In traditional East Asian diplomacy, the vertical placement of a name on a page indicated rank; placing a name lower than usual was a deliberate sign of disrespect or inferiority</note>. The Korean envoy had not protested this insult as his duty required, yet the King had promoted him in rank anyway. The various officials praised Choi for his courage in speaking out about this.

In 1612, while he was serving as Chief Justice, King Gwanghaegun <note>original: "Kwang-hai"</note> attempted to strip the Queen Dowager <note>Queen Inmok (1584–1632), the stepmother of Gwanghaegun</note> of her royal rank. She was not the King's biological mother, as he was the son of a concubine, but Yu-won pleaded with him through tears not to do such an illegal and unnatural thing. Nevertheless, the King overrode all opposition and followed his own disrespectful <note>original: "unfilial"</note> will. Throughout these events, Choi Yu-won proved himself to be a good and just man. Even as a young man, he used to tell his companions, “Death is terrifying, but it is better to die for the sake of righteousness and honor than to live in disgrace.” Another of his sayings was: “The goal of all study is the development of character; if it does not result in that, it is useless.”
