Yu-won used a knife to cut flesh from his thigh until the blood flowed, and with this, he prepared his medicinal potion <note>original: "magic dose"; in Korean folklore, cutting flesh from one's own body to feed a sick parent was considered the ultimate act of filial piety and was believed to have life-saving properties</note>.]

### *The Story*

In the old days, there was a high-ranking official who was serving as the Palace Secretary. One morning, while he was getting ready for work, he put on his ceremonial robes. Because it was still quite early, he leaned against his arm-rest for a moment and fell asleep. 

He began to dream, and in the dream, he imagined he was on horseback, traveling to his office. As he was crossing the bridge at the entrance to East Palace Street, he suddenly saw his mother walking toward him. He immediately got off his horse, bowed, and asked, "Mother, why are you walking like this? Why are you on foot instead of traveling in a sedan chair?"

She replied, "I have already left the world of the living. Things where I am are not the same as they are for you, and so I must walk."

The secretary asked, "Please, where are you going?"

She replied, "One of our servants lives in Yong-san, and they are currently holding a shamanistic ritual <note>original: "witches' prayer service"; refers to a *gut*, a Korean shamanic ceremony involving offerings to spirits and ancestors</note>. I am going there to share in the sacrificial offerings."

"But," the secretary said, "we hold many ancestral memorial services <note>original: "sacrificial days"; refers to *jesa*, the Confucian tradition of honoring ancestors with food and prayer</note> at our own home, those for the—"

<summary>This page concludes a story about Choi Yu-won’s filial sacrifice and begins a new narrative about a Palace Secretary who meets the spirit of his deceased mother in a dream while she is on her way to a shamanistic ritual.</summary>
<keywords>Choi Yu-won, Palace Secretary, filial piety, shamanism, ghosts, dreams, ancestral worship, Yong-san</keywords>

"...four seasons, as well as on the first and fifteenth of each month. Why do you go to a servant's house and not to mine?"

His mother replied, "Your offerings <note>original: "sacrifices"; referring to *jesa*, the Confucian ritual of offering food to ancestors</note> are of no interest to me; I prefer the prayers of the shamans <note>original: "witches"</note>. Without a medium to speak for us, we spirits find no satisfaction. I am in a hurry," she said, "and cannot wait any longer." With that, she said her farewells and was gone.

The secretary woke up with a start, but he felt as though he had truly witnessed these events.

He then called a servant and ordered him to go immediately to a specific house in Yong-san <note>a district in modern-day Seoul</note> to tell a certain servant there to come that night without fail. "Go quickly," the secretary said, "so that you return before I must go to the Palace." Then he sat down to reflect on the dream.

In a short while, the servant had gone and returned. It was not yet full daylight, and because it was cold, the servant did not go inside immediately but went first to the kitchen to warm his hands by the fire. A fellow servant was there and asked him, "Have you had something to drink?"

He replied, "They are holding a large shamanic ritual <note>original: "witch business"</note> at Yong-san. While the *mudang* <note>original: "mutang"; a Korean shaman, traditionally female, who acts as a bridge to the spirit world</note> was performing, she claimed that the spirit possessing her was the mother of our master. When I appeared, she called me by name and said, 'This is a servant from our house.' Then she—"

He called in the servant and questioned him more closely, and he felt more certain than ever that his mother's spirit had indeed gone that morning to take part in the *gut* <note>original: "koot"; a Korean shamanic ritual performed by a mudang to commune with or appease spirits</note>. He then called the *mudang* <note>original: "mutang"; a Korean shaman, typically female</note> and, on behalf of his mother's spirit, provided a great offering for her. From then on, he performed ancestral rites <note>original: "sacrificed"</note> for her four times a year, at the change of each season.

### XXXIII

## THE FEARLESS CAPTAIN

**THERE** was once a soldier named Yi Man-ji <note>original: "Yee Man-ji"</note> from Yeongnam <note>original: "Yong-nam"; the southeastern region of Korea</note>, a strong and muscular fellow who was as brave as a lion. He had green eyes and a fierce expression <note>original: "terrible countenance"</note>. He frequently said, “Fear! What is fear?” One day while he was at home, a sudden rainstorm broke out, accompanied by flashes of lightning and heavy claps of thunder. During the storm, a large ball of fire <note>likely ball lightning</note> came tumbling into his house. It rolled across the porch <note>original: "verandah"</note>, through the rooms, into the kitchen, out into the yard, and then into the servants’ quarters. It bounced back and forth several times. Its blazing light and the crashing noise that followed made it a terrifying sight.

Yi sat on the outer porch, completely undisturbed. He thought to himself, “I have done nothing wrong, so why should I fear the lightning?” A moment later, a bolt struck the large elm tree in front of the house and smashed it to pieces. The rain then stopped, and the thunder ceased as well.

Yi turned to see how his family was faring,

<summary>This page introduces Captain Yi Man-ji, a soldier famous for his bravery, who remains remarkably calm when a ball of lightning rolls through his home during a violent storm. His fearlessness is rooted in his belief that a man with a clear conscience has nothing to fear from nature's fury.</summary>

<keywords>Yi Man-ji, Yeongnam, courage, ball lightning, storm, soldier, Korean folklore, bravery</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the story of Yi Man-ji, following the moment a ball of lightning terrified his family but left him unmoved.</meta>

-># THE FEARLESS CAPTAIN<-

...and found them all lying unconscious. With great difficulty, he managed to revive them. Later that year, however, they all fell ill and died. Yi then moved to Seoul and became a Captain of the Right Guard. Shortly afterward, he was sent to North Hamgyong Province <note>original: "North Ham-kyong Province"; a province in the far northeast of Korea</note>. There, he took a second wife and settled down. All of his predecessors in that post had died because of malevolent spirits <note>original: "goblin influences"</note>. Because disaster had struck them while they were living in the official residence, their successors had chosen to live in village houses instead.

Yi, however, was determined to overcome all fear and return to the old official quarters, which he repaired extensively.

One night, while his wife was in the inner room, he sat alone in the public office with a lamp burning before him. During the second watch <note>The second watch was the period between 9:00 PM and 11:00 PM.</note> or thereabouts, a strange-looking object emerged from the inner quarters. It resembled a tree stump wrapped in black sackcloth. It had no clear outline or definite shape; it came hopping along and sat down directly in front of Yi Man-ji. Two other objects, shaped exactly like the first, followed behind it. The three then sat in a row before Yi, inching closer and closer to him. Yi backed away until he was pressed against the wall and could go no further. Then he said, “Who are you, anyway? What kind of devil are you, that you dare to crowd me so in my...”

office? If you have any complaint or matter to set right, say so, and I'll see to it."

The middle devil said in reply, "I'm hungry, I'm hungry, I'm hungry."

Yi answered, "Hungry, are you? Very well, now just move back and I'll have food prepared for you in abundance." He then repeated a magic formula that he had learned and snapped his fingers. The three devils seemed to be afraid of this. Then Man-ji suddenly clenched his fist and struck a blow at the first devil. It dodged, however, most deftly, and he missed, hitting the floor with a resounding blow <note>original: "sounding blow"</note> that cut his hand.

Then they all shouted, "We'll go, we'll go, since you treat guests this way!" At once they rushed out <note>original: "bundled out"</note> of the room and disappeared.

On the following day, he had oxen slaughtered and a sacrifice offered to these devils, and they returned no more.

*Note.*—People have been killed by goblins <note>original: "goblins"; these are likely *dokkaebi*, mischievous or malevolent nature spirits in Korean folklore</note>. This is not so much due to the fact that goblins are wicked as it is to the fact that people are afraid of them. Many died in North Hamgyong <note>original: "North Ham-kyong"</note>, but those who were brave and struck them down with a knife <note>original: "clove them with a knife"</note> or a blow survived. If they had been afraid, they too would have died.

# XXXIV THE KING OF YOM-NA (HELL) <note>Yom-na: Derived from the Sanskrit "Yama," this is the King of the Underworld in Buddhist and Korean traditional belief who judges the dead.</note>

->[Pak Chom was one of the Royal Censors and died during the Japanese invasion of 1592 <note>original: "Japanese War of 1592"; this conflict is commonly known as the Imjin War.</note>.]<-

### *The Story*

In Yeonan <note>original: "Yon-nan"</note> County, in Hwanghae <note>original: "Whang-hai"</note> Province, there was a certain scholar <note>original: "literary graduate"; a man who had passed the civil service examinations</note> whose name I have forgotten. He fell ill one day and remained in his room, leaning helplessly against his armrest. Suddenly, several spirit soldiers <note>These are likely the *Jeoseung Saja*, the messengers of death who escort souls to the afterlife.</note> appeared to him, saying, “The King of the Underworld <note>original: "Governor of the lower hell"</note> has ordered your arrest.” They bound him with a chain around his neck and led him away. They traveled for many hundreds of miles <note>The original likely refers to *ri*, a traditional unit of distance; 100 *ri* is roughly 25 miles.</note> and at last reached a place that had a very high wall. The spirits then took him inside the walls and traveled on for a long distance.

Inside this enclosure was a massive structure so tall it seemed to reach the heavens. They arrived at the gate, and the spirits who held him led him inside. When they entered the inner courtyard, they forced him to lie face down on the ground.

Looking up, he saw what appeared to be a king seated on a high throne.

<summary>A scholar from Yeonan County is arrested by spirit soldiers and taken to the high-walled palace of the Underworld to face the King of Hell.</summary>

<keywords>Pak Chom, Yom-na, King of Hell, Underworld, Yeonan, Hwanghae, spirit soldiers, Jeoseung Saja, Imjin War, Korean folklore</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of a scholar from Yeonan who has been summoned to the Underworld by mistake.</meta>

[The King sat] on a throne; grouped about him on each side were attendant officers. There were also dozens of secretaries and soldiers rushing back and forth on urgent errands. The King’s appearance was terrifying, and his commands were so powerful they filled everyone with awe. The scholar <note>original: "graduate"; a man who had passed the civil service examinations</note> felt sweat break out on his back, and he did not dare to look up. 

After a short while, a secretary stepped forward and stood in front of the raised platform <note>original: "dais"</note> to deliver the King's commands. The King asked, “Where do you come from? What is your name? How old are you? What do you do for a living? Tell me the truth now, and do not lie <note>original: "dissembling"</note>.”

The scholar, frightened to death, replied, “My family name <note>original: "clan name"</note> is [Name], and my given name is [Name]. I am [this many] years old, and my family has lived for several generations at Yeonan <note>original: "Yon-nan"</note>, in Hwanghae <note>original: "Whang-hai"</note> Province. I am simple and poorly equipped by nature, so I have not done anything special with my life. However, I have heard all my life that if you recite your prayer beads <note>Buddhist prayer beads, or *yeomju*, are used to keep count while chanting mantras or the Buddha's name.</note> with love and pity in your heart, you will escape hell. Because of this, I have spent my time calling on the name of the Buddha and giving alms <note>charitable gifts to the poor or to religious orders</note>.”

The secretary, hearing this, went at once and reported it to the King. After some time, he came back with a message, saying, “Come up closer to the steps. You are not the person we intended to summon. It happens that you share the same name as another man, and you have thus been wrongly arrested. You may go now.”

### THE KING OF YOM-NA (HELL) <note>original: "Yom-na"; referring to Yeomra-daewang, the Korean name for Yama, the Buddhist and Taoist King of the Dead and Judge of the Underworld.</note>

The scholar joined his hands and made a deep bow. Again, the secretary delivered a message from the King, saying, “When I lived on earth, my house was in a certain neighborhood <note>original: "ward"</note> of Seoul <note>Seoul was known as Hanyang during the Joseon Dynasty (1392–1910).</note>. When you return, I want you to deliver a message for me. I have been here a long time, and the outer coat I wear has been worn to shreds. Ask my family to send me a new outer coat. If you do this, I will be very grateful, so make sure you do not forget.”

The scholar replied, “I will certainly deliver Your Majesty’s message exactly as you have commanded. However, the ways of the two worlds—the world of darkness and the world of light—are so different that when I give the message, those who hear it will say I am talking nonsense. I will tell them exactly what you said, but what should I do if they refuse to listen? I need some evidence to prove my story.”

The King answered, “Your words are very true. This will help you: When I was on earth,” he said, “one of the head buttons <note>A head button (gwanja) was a decorative bead used to fasten a headband (manggeon). These buttons were made of materials like gold, jade, or horn to indicate a man's social and bureaucratic rank.</note> that I wore had a chipped edge, and I hid it in the third volume of the *Book of History* <note>The *Book of History* (Shujing) is one of the Five Classics of ancient Chinese literature, a fundamental text for any Korean scholar during this period.</note>. I am the only one who knows about it; no one else in the world does. If you provide this as proof, they will listen to you.”

The scholar replied, “That will be sufficient. But how should I proceed if they actually make the new coat?”

After hearing this request, the servant returned and reported it to his master, who, a short time later, ordered the scholar to be brought in. Upon entering, he saw two gentlemen who appeared to be high-ranking officials. They asked him to sit down and then questioned him about who he was and what he had to say.

# THE KING OF YEOMRA (HELL) <note>original: "YOM-NA"; referring to Yeomra-daewang, the Buddhist and Taoist deity who judges the dead.</note>

He replied, “I am a student living in Yeonan County, Hwanghae Province <note>original: "Yon-nan County, Whang-hai Province."</note>. On such and such a day, I died and went into the other world, where your honorable father gave me a specific task to perform.”

The two sons listened for a short time and then, without waiting to hear all that he had to say, grew very angry and began to scold him, saying, “How dare such a ragged scarecrow like you come into our house and say such things? This is nothing but nonsense you are talking. Throw him out!” they shouted to the servants.

The scholar, however, called back, saying, “I have proof; listen to me. If it fails, then you may throw me out.”

One of the two asked, “What possible proof could you have?” Then the scholar told them, with great exactness and care, the story of the head button <note>The scholar is referring to a "gwanja," a decorative button used on a man’s headband (manggeon) to indicate rank or status.</note>.

The two, in astonishment over this, had the book taken down and examined; and sure enough, in Volume III of the *Book of History* <note>original: "Book of History"; likely referring to a specific classic or family record where the item was hidden.</note> was the button he had described. Not a single detail was wrong. It proved to be a button that they had missed after the death of their father, and for which they had searched in vain.

Accepting the message as true now, they all began a period of mourning.

The women of the family also called for the scholar and asked him specifically about what he had seen in the afterlife. Then they made the outer coat as instructed, chose an auspicious day, and—

<summary>The scholar proves his story by revealing the location of a missing headband button hidden in a book; the family, convinced, begins mourning rituals for their father.</summary>

<keywords>Yeomra, Underworld, afterlife, Yeonan, Hwanghae, proof, headband button, mourning rituals, Pak Oo</keywords>

Three days after the sacrifice, the scholar dreamed—and the Pak family dreamed as well—that the King of Hades <note>original: "King of Hades"; referring to Yeomra-daewang, the Buddhist and Taoist deity who judges the dead.</note> had come and given his thanks to each one of them for the coat. They hosted the scholar at their home for a long time, treating him with great respect, and they remained firm friends for the rest of their lives.

Pak Oo was a great-grandson of Minister Pak Chom. While he held office, he was honest and just, and he was highly honored by the people. When he was the Mayor of Haeju <note>original: "Hai-ju"; a city in what is now North Korea.</note>, a dispute arose between him and the Governor, which also proved that Pak was an honest man.

When I was in Haeju, a graduate named Choi Yu-chom told me this story.

<header>XXXV</header>

### HONG'S EXPERIENCES IN HADES <note>original: "Hades"; referring to the Korean Underworld or Jiok, ruled by the Ten Kings of Hell.</note>

Hong Nai-pom was a military graduate who was born in 1561 and lived in the city of Pyongyang <note>original: "Pyeng-yang"</note>. He passed his military examination in 1603, and in 1637, he reached the Third Degree of rank. In 1643, when he was eighty-two years old, his son, Sonn, petitioned <note>original: "memorialized"; to submit a formal petition to a monarch.</note> the King, asking that his father be granted a rank appropriate for his great age. 

At that time, a man named Han Hong-kil was the Chief of the Royal Secretaries <note>original: "Royal Secretaries"; likely referring to the Seungjeongwon, the King's executive secretariat.</note>, and he refused to pass the request on to His Majesty. However, in 1644, when the Crown Prince was returning from his exile in China, he passed through Pyongyang. Sonn took advantage of this opportunity to present the same request to the Crown Prince. His Highness accepted it and brought it to the King's attention. As a result, Hong was granted the rank of Second Degree.

Upon receiving the honor, Hong remarked, "I shall die this year," and shortly afterward, he passed away.

In 1594, Hong had fallen ill with typhus fever <note>original: "typhus fever"; likely a general term for an epidemic fever prevalent during the Imjin War period.</note>. After ten days of suffering, he died. His family prepared his body for burial and placed it in a coffin.

Then the friends and relatives left, and his wife remained alone in charge. Suddenly, the body turned itself over and fell with a thud to the ground. The woman, frightened, fainted away, and the other members of the family came rushing to her help. From this time on, the body began to function again, and Hong lived.

He said, "In my dream, I went to a certain realm—a place of great terror where many people were standing around. There were awful ogres, some of them wearing bulls' heads <note>original: "bulls' heads"; likely referring to Go-du, the ox-headed guardians of the underworld in East Asian mythology.</note>, and some with the faces of wild beasts. They crowded around and jumped and pounced toward me from all directions. A scribe dressed in black sat on a platform and addressed me, saying, 'There are three religions on earth: Confucianism, Buddhism, and Taoism. According to Buddhism, you know that heaven and hell are places that decide between a person’s good and evil deeds. You have always been a blasphemer of the Buddha and a denier of a future life, acting always as though you knew everything, blustering and storming. You are now to be sent to hell, and ten thousand *kalpas* <note>original: "kalpas"; an era in Buddhist cosmology representing a vast, nearly infinite period of time.</note> will not see you out of it.'

"Then two or three guards carrying spears came and took me off. I screamed, 'You are wrong! I am innocently condemned.' Just at that moment, a certain Buddha, with a face of shining gold, appeared..."

The next hell was marked “Liars.” In that hell, I saw an iron pillar several yards high with great stones placed before it. The offenders were called forward and made to kneel before the pillar.

The next hell had an inscription that read, "Deceivers." I saw dozens <note>original: "many scores"; a score is twenty.</note> of people there. Hong’s mind was filled with pride because of this, and he frequently boasted of it. His lifespan and his attainment of the Second Degree rank <note>A high level of achievement in the civil service examinations of the Joseon dynasty, which determined a man's social and political standing.</note> happened exactly as the Buddha had predicted.

His experience, unfortunately, was used as a way to deceive people, because the Superior Man <note>original: "Superior Man"; a Confucian ideal of the perfected, virtuous person who focuses on practical ethics and social harmony rather than the supernatural.</note> does not speak of such strange and miraculous things.

Yi Tan, a Chinese man from the Song Kingdom <note>The Song Dynasty (960–1279), a period of great cultural and philosophical development in China.</note>, used to say, “If there is no heaven, then there is no heaven; but if there is one, only the Superior Man can reach it. If there is no hell, then there is no hell; but if there is one, the wicked must inherit it.”

If we examine Hong’s story, though it looks like a tall tale <note>original: "yarn"</note> intended to deceive the world, it is actually a story meant to inspire right action. I, Im Bang, have recorded it just as Toi-chi <note>Likely a reference to Han Yu (known by the style name Tuizhi or Toi-chi), a famous Tang Dynasty scholar-official who often wrote about morality and criticized superstition.</note> did, saying, “Do not find fault with the story itself, but learn the lesson it teaches.”

### XXXVI

## HAUNTED HOUSES

There once lived a man in Seoul called Yi Chang, who often told the following story as his own experience: He was poor and had no home of his own, so he frequently lived in quarters lent to him by others. When he was desperate, he even went into haunted houses and lived there. Once, after failing to find a place to stay, he heard of one such house in Ink Town <note>Ink Town (original: Muk-tong) was a district in central Seoul known for producing ink-sticks.</note> (one of the wards of Seoul), at the foot of South Mountain <note>Known as Namsan, a prominent mountain in the heart of Seoul.</note>. The house had been haunted for generations and was now left vacant. Chang investigated the matter and finally decided to take possession of it.

First, to find out whether it was really haunted or not, he called his elder brothers, Hugh and Haw <note>These are likely the translator's Anglicized phonetic versions of Korean names such as Hyu and Ho.</note>, and five or six of his relatives, and had them help clean it out and sleep there. The house had one upper room that was securely locked. Looking through a crack in the door, they saw that there was a spirit tablet chair <note>A small, ceremonial chair or stand used to hold a wooden tablet inscribed with an ancestor's name during Confucian rites.</note> and a stand for it in the room. There was also an old harp <note>Likely a *geomungo* or *gayageum*, traditional Korean stringed instruments.</note> without any strings, a pair of worn shoes, and some...

<summary>A poor man named Yi Chang moves into a notoriously haunted house in Seoul's Ink Town and discovers a mysterious locked room containing ritual objects and old belongings.</summary>

But the sound of the music and the partying continued until morning. The group left at daybreak, withdrew from the place, and never dared to enter it again.

The group watching from the inner room had witnessed all of this. When daylight came, they examined the area. They looked through the cracks in the floor but saw only an old, worn-out sieve and a few discarded brooms. They went behind the house and found another old broom shoved into the chimney. They ordered a servant to gather the items and burn them <note>It was a common Korean belief that discarded household objects, especially those stained with human blood or sweat like brooms and sieves, could transform into *dokkaebi* (goblins).</note>. The dogs lay exactly where they were all day, and they neither ate nor moved. Some members of the group...

There is another story about a scholar <note>original: "graduate" — a person who had passed the civil service examinations, a significant rank in Joseon-era society.</note> who was homeless and moved into a haunted house in Ink Town <note>original: "Ink Town" — likely referring to Muk-tong, a neighborhood in old Seoul.</note>. It was said to have a tower where mysterious sounds could be heard. They opened the door, broke the window, and took out an old harp, a spirit chair <note>original: "spirit chair" — a small ceremonial sedan chair used to transport the tablets or souls of the deceased during funeral rites.</note>, shoes, and walking sticks, and tried to burn them. Before the fire had finished burning the items, one of the servants suddenly collapsed and died. Seeing this, the scholar was filled with fear and dismay; he put out the fire, returned the items to their places, and left the house.

Another homeless man tried to stay there as well. During the night, a woman wearing a blue skirt came down from the loft and acted in a strange and eerie manner. When the man saw this, he gathered his belongings and fled.

# XXXVII

## IM, THE HUNTER

[**IM KYONG-UP.**—One of Korea’s most famous generals, who fought on behalf of China in 1628 against the Manchus. He is worshipped today in many parts of Korea <note>Im Kyong-up (1594–1646) is a legendary hero in Korean history known for his military prowess and loyalty. In the centuries following his death, he became a prominent deity in Korean shamanism, often invoked for protection.</note>.]

### *The Story*

When General Im Kyong-up was young, he lived in the town of Tallai. In those days, he loved the hunt <note>original: "the chase"</note> and constantly practiced riding and hunting. Once, he went on an excursion to track deer in the Wollak Mountains. He carried only a sword and conducted the hunt on foot. 

In his pursuit of the animal, he traveled as far as Taebaek Mountain <note>original: "Tai-paik Mountain" — the main mountain range that forms the "spine" of the Korean Peninsula.</note>. Night overtook him there, and the path was hidden in the darkness. There were yawning chasms and great peaks <note>original: "horns" — used here to describe sharp, horn-like mountain crags.</note> and cliffs on all sides. While he was in a state of confusion, he met a woodcutter <note>original: "woodman"</note> and asked him where the road was and which way he should go. The woodcutter directed him to a cliff opposite, saying,

<summary>The story introduces the youth of General Im Kyong-up, a legendary Korean figure, who becomes lost at night during a solo deer hunt in the rugged Taebaek Mountains.</summary>

<keywords>Im Kyong-up, hunting, Taebaek Mountain, Wollak Mountains, woodcutter, Korean general, folklore</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of the young Im Kyong-up (later a famous general) who has become lost while hunting in the Taebaek Mountains. He has just encountered a mysterious woodcutter who points him toward shelter.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>

## IM, THE HUNTER

"...There is a house." Im heard this and crossed over to the farther ridge. On approaching more closely, he found a great tiled mansion standing alone, without a single other house near it. He went in through the main gateway but found everything quiet and dark, with no one in sight. It was a vacant house, evidently deserted. 

After traveling all day in the hills, Im was full of fears and creepy feelings. He viewed the place with mistrust, fearing that there might be hill goblins or tree devils <note>original: "hill goblins... or tree devils" — likely referring to *dokkaebi*, mischievous nature spirits or mountain demons in Korean folklore.</note> lurking within. But a moment later, someone opened a room door and shouted, "Are you sleeping here? Have you had something to eat?"

Im looked and discovered that it was the same person who had directed him on his way. He replied, "I have not eaten anything and am hungry." 

So the man opened the wall box <note>original: "wall box" — a *byeok-jang*, a traditional Korean built-in cupboard or closet set into the wall.</note> and brought him wine and meat. Since he was exceedingly hungry, Im ate it all. They sat down to talk together, and after a little while, the woodcutter <note>original: "woodman"</note> got up, opened the box once more, and took a great sword from it. 

Im asked, "What is that you have? Do you intend to kill me?"

The woodcutter laughed and said, "No, no; but tonight there is something happening worth seeing. Will you come with me and not be afraid?"

Im said, "Of course I am not afraid; I want to see."

It was then about midnight, and the woodcutter <note>original: "woodman"</note>, with the sword in his hand, took Im and went to one side through a succession of gates that seemed never-ending. At last, they came to a place where lights were reflected on a pond of water. There was a high pavilion <note>original: "pavilion" — likely a *nu-gak*, a traditional Korean multi-story open-air wooden structure used for leisure and views.</note> apparently in the middle of the lake, and the lights came from inside it. There were sounds, too, of laughter and talking that came from the upper story <note>original: "storey"</note>. Through the sliding doors, he could distinguish two people seated together. There was another pavilion to the right of the lake and a large tree near it, which the woodcutter told Im to climb.

"When you get well up," said he, "take your belt, tie yourself fast to the trunk, and keep perfectly still."

Im climbed the tree as directed and made himself secure. From this vantage point, he looked intently, and the first thing he saw was the woodcutter give a leap that cleared the lake and landed him in the pavilion. At once, he ascended to the upper story, and now Im could distinguish three persons sitting, talking, and laughing. After drinking, he heard the woodcutter say to his companion <note>original: "neighbour"</note>, "We have made our wager; now let's see it out." The man replied, "Let's do so." Then both arose, came down to the entrance, and leaped <note>original: "vaulted off"</note> into mid-air, where they disappeared from sight.

Watching this from the treetop where he was positioned, his bones turned cold and his hair stood on end. He did not know what to do. Chills ran through him and goosebumps <note>original: "goose-flesh"</note> covered his skin; it was only after a long time that he could regain his composure. He climbed down from the tree and the woodcutter met him, suddenly tucked him under his arm, and leaped <note>original: "vaulted"</note> over into the pavilion. There he met a beautiful woman with hair like fleecy clouds. Before the fight, the woman’s voice had clearly been full of laughter, but now she was overcome with grief and tears.

The woodcutter spoke harshly to her, saying, "Don't you know that you, a wicked woman, have caused the death of a great man?" Im replied, "What I have seen tonight, I am at

I will teach you.”

Im stayed for six days and learned how to use the sword.

### XXXVIII

## THE MAGIC INVASION OF SEOUL

A gentleman from Seoul was crossing the Han River in a boat one day. While crossing, he dozed off for a moment, fell asleep, and had a dream. In his dream, he met a man who had arched <note>original: "Gothic." In early translations, "Gothic" was often used to describe sharp, slanting, or "bird-wing" eyebrows typical of warrior portraits.</note> eyebrows and almond eyes, whose face was as red as ripened dates, and who stood nearly thirteen feet tall <note>original: "eight cubits and a span." A cubit is roughly 18 inches and a span is about 9 inches.</note>. He was dressed in green and had a long beard that reached down to his belt. He was a man of majestic appearance, with a great sword at his side, and he rode a red horse.

He asked the gentleman to open his hand, which he did, and then the majestic <note>original: "august"</note> stranger quickly marked it with a pen as the sign of the God of War <note>This refers to Guan Yu (known in Korea as Kwan-u), a deified Chinese general from the *Romance of the Three Kingdoms*. He is a popular figure in Korean folk religion, traditionally depicted with a red face, a long beard, and a green robe.</note>. He said, “When you cross the river, do not go directly to Seoul, but wait at the landing. Seven horses will soon appear, loaded with woven baskets <note>original: "network hampers"</note>, all traveling toward the capital. You must call to the horsemen, open your hand, and show them the sign. When they see it, they will all commit suicide in your very presence. After that, you are to take their loads and pile them up, but—”

<vocab>Seoul, Han River, Gothic eyebrows, God of War, network hampers</vocab>

<summary>A traveler on the Han River has a vision of the God of War (Guan Yu), who marks his hand with a divine sign and instructs him to intercept seven mysterious horsemen heading toward Seoul.</summary>
<keywords>Guan Yu, Han River, Seoul, prophecy, God of War, omen, dream vision</keywords>

“...don’t look into them. Then you are to go at once and report the matter to the Palace and have them all burned. The matter is of immense importance, so do not fail in the slightest detail.”

The gentleman woke with a start, terrified. He looked at his hand and there, indeed, was the strange mark. Not only that, but the ink had not yet dried. He was astonished beyond measure but did as the dream had instructed and waited on the river’s bank. In a short while, just as he had been advised, seven horses arrived carrying seven loads, coming from the far-distant South. There were attendants in charge, and one man wearing an official's robe <note>original: "official coat." This likely refers to a *gwanbok*, the formal attire of a government official.</note> followed behind. When they had crossed the river, the gentleman called them to him and said, “I have something to say to you; come close to me.” These men, unsuspecting though looking somewhat frightened, gathered around. He then showed them his hand with the mark and asked them if they knew what it was. When they saw it, the man in the official robe immediately turned and, with one bound, jumped over the cliff into the river. The eight or nine others who accompanied the loads likewise all rushed after him and dashed into the water.

The scholar then called the boatmen and explained to them that the things in the baskets <note>original: "hampers"</note> were dangerous, and that he would have to make it

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

The scholar explained that the matter must be made known to the Palace, and that in the meantime, the boatmen were to keep a close guard. He warned them not to touch the baskets or even look inside them.

He hurried as fast as possible and reported the situation to the Ministry of War <note>original: "Board of War." This refers to the *Byeongjo*, the government department responsible for military affairs during the Joseon Dynasty.</note>. The Ministry sent an official to have the loads brought into Seoul. Then, as had been instructed, the baskets were piled high with wood and set on fire. As the fire grew, the baskets <note>original: "hampers"</note> burst open, and countless tiny figures of men and horses—each only about an inch long—came tumbling out.

When the officials saw this, they were paralyzed with fear; their hearts seemed to stop and their tongues hung out in shock <note>original: "tongues lolled out." This is a traditional idiom expressing extreme astonishment or horror.</note>. In a short while, however, the baskets were entirely consumed by the flames.

These figures were the creation of a magician and were intended for a massive supernatural invasion of Seoul, which was only stopped because they had been warned by Kwan <note>original: "Kwan." This refers to Guan Yu, the deified Chinese general who was widely worshipped in Korea as the God of War (*Gwan-wang* or *Gwan-u*).</note>.

From that time on, the people of Seoul began to offer faithful sacrifices to the God of War, for had he not saved the city?

### XXXIX

## THE AWFUL LITTLE GOBLIN

There was once a celebration in the home of a nobleman of Seoul, and a feast was prepared to which all the family’s friends were invited. There was a great crowd of men and women. In front of the women’s quarters <note>original: "inner quarters" or *anchae*. In traditional Korean architecture, the *anchae* was the residential area reserved for the women of the household.</note> there suddenly appeared an unkempt, ugly-looking boy about fifteen years of age. The host and guests, thinking he was a laborer <note>original: "coolie." This historical term referred to an unskilled laborer or porter.</note> who had followed some visitor’s entourage, did not ask any specific questions about him. However, one of the women guests, seeing him in the inner quarters, sent a servant to scold him and send him away. The boy did not move, so the servant said to him, “Who are you, anyway, and who did you come with? Why have you entered the women’s quarters, and why do you refuse to leave even when told to go?”

The boy, however, stood perfectly still, just as he had been, without saying a word in reply.

The company looked at him with suspicion and began to ask one another who he belonged to and who he had come with.

<vocab>Seoul, nobleman, goblin, laborer, women’s quarters</vocab>

<summary>During a nobleman's feast in Seoul, a mysterious and silent ugly boy appears in the women's quarters, refusing to leave or identify himself. The guests and hosts are confused by his presence, initially mistaking him for a servant's attendant.</summary>
<keywords>Seoul, goblin, social customs, domestic life, mystery, Joseon Dynasty</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of "The Awful Little Goblin." After appearing uninvited in the women's quarters of a nobleman's house, the mysterious boy refuses to speak or leave, leading to a confrontation with the household and guests.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

...asking who he had come with. Again they had the servant ask him, but still there was no reply. The women then grew very angry and ordered him to be thrown out. Several of them grabbed him and tried to pull him away, but he was like a solid rock, rooted in the earth and absolutely immovable. In a helpless rage, they informed the men of the situation.

When the men heard this, they sent several strong servants who all grabbed him at once, but he did not budge an inch. They asked, “Who are you, anyway?” but he gave no answer. Enraged, the crowd then sent ten strong men with ropes to tie him up, but like a massive mountain, he remained so still that they realized he could not be moved by human strength alone.

One guest remarked, “But he is human, too; why can’t he be moved?” They then sent five or six giant men with clubs to beat him to pieces, and they struck him with all their might. It looked as though he would be crushed like an eggshell, and the sound of their pounding was like rolling thunder. But just as before, he did not flinch or even blink.

Then the crowd began to feel afraid, saying, “This is not a man, but a god <note>original: "god." In this context, the guests likely believe the boy is a *shin* (a deity or spirit) or a powerful *dokkaebi* (a Korean goblin with supernatural powers).</note>.” They all entered the courtyard together and began to bow before him, joining their hands and pleading <note>original: "supplicating."</note> earnestly. They continued doing this for a long time.

<summary>A mysterious boy at a feast proves to be supernatural when he remains physically immovable and unharmed despite the efforts of strong men and violent beatings. Terrified, the guests conclude he is a deity and begin to worship him in the courtyard.</summary>

<keywords>supernatural, immovable, dokkaebi, deity, Joseon Dynasty, folk magic, Korean folklore</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of "The Awful Little Goblin," describing the tragic aftermath of the guests' encounter with the supernatural boy.</meta>

<page-num>193</page-num>

<header>THE AWFUL LITTLE GOBLIN</header>

At last, the boy gave a sarcastic smile, turned around, walked out of the gate, and disappeared.

The guests, terrified out of their wits, ended the feast immediately. From that day forward, everyone in that house became ill, including the host and his guests. Those who had scolded the boy, those who had tied him with ropes, and those who had beaten him all died within a few days. Other members of the group also contracted typhus <note>A severe infectious disease often occurring in epidemics, historically associated with war and famine.</note> and similar illnesses, and they died as well.

It was commonly believed that the boy was the Too-uk Spirit <note>original: "Too-uk Spirit." Known in Korean folklore as the *Du-eok-sini*, this is a particularly violent and powerful type of goblin or monster known for its physical strength and its ability to cause sudden illness or death if offended.</note>, but we cannot say for certain. Strange indeed!

*Note.*—When the time comes for a family <note>original: "clan"</note> to vanish from the earth, disaster strikes them. Even if a powerful spirit enters during a feast, if the guests had followed the advice of Confucius to "Respect spirits, but keep your distance," <note>original: "Be reverent and distant." A famous teaching from the *Analects* of Confucius regarding the proper attitude toward spiritual beings—treating them with respect but not seeking intimacy or conflict with them.</note> instead of insulting him and making him more malicious than ever, they might have escaped. Still, demons and men were never intended to live together.

# XL GOD'S WAY

In a certain town, there lived a man with a fierce and uncontrollable temper who, in moments of anger, used to beat his mother. One day, after being beaten, his mother cried out, "Oh, God, why do You not strike down this wicked man who beats his mother?"

After the beating was over, the son tucked his sickle <note>A hand-held agricultural tool with a curved blade used for harvesting grain.</note> into his belt and walked slowly to the fields, where he was employed by a neighbor to harvest buckwheat. The day was fine, and the sky was beautifully clear. Suddenly, a small dark cloud appeared in the middle of the sky, and a short time later, the entire sky turned black. Violent thunder followed, and rain began to fall. The villagers looked out toward the field, where the flashes of lightning were particularly intense. They thought they saw a man there, holding up his sickle as if trying to fend off the lightning bolts. When the storm had cleared, they went to investigate, and lo! <note>An archaic exclamation used to draw attention to a surprising or amazing event.</note> They found the man who

### XLI

## THE OLD MAN IN THE DREAM

KWON JAI <note>Kwon Je (1387–1445) was a prominent scholar-official during the early Joseon Dynasty.</note> was a man of high rank and advanced age. He was, however, very fond of sports and various kinds of pleasure. One night he had a dream in which an old man came to him, bowed low, and said through his tears, “Sir, Minister Hong intends to kill me and all my descendants <note>original: "posterity"</note>. Please save me, won’t you?”

Kwon asked, “How can I save you?” 

The old man replied, “Hong will certainly ask Your Excellency to help him. Please refuse to join him <note>original: "Desist from it"</note>, for if you do, Hong will give up his plan, and I and my family shall live.”

A little later there was a knock at the door. Kwon woke up and asked, “Who is there?” It was Hong. That day, Hong had planned a trip to Lotus Lake to fish for turtles, and he had come specifically to invite Kwon to go with him.

<summary>High official Kwon Jai receives a dream-visitation from an old man (a turtle spirit) begging for protection from Minister Hong's fishing trip. Kwon wakes to find Hong at his door, ready to start the very hunt mentioned in the dream.</summary>

<keywords>Kwon Jai, Minister Hong, Lotus Lake, prophetic dream, turtles, Korean folklore, animal spirits, mercy</keywords>

Then Kwon realized that the turtle had appeared to him in a dream in the form of an old man. Because of this, he declined the invitation, claiming he was ill. I learned later that Hong also did not go <note>By refusing to participate, Kwon successfully prevented the hunt, as the turtle spirit had predicted.</note>.

<header>XLII</header>

## THE PERFECT PRIEST

There was once a priest named Namnu who had mastered the Buddhist teachings. Whenever he had clothes of his own, he would gladly undress and give them to those who were cold. His spirit was gentle, with no hard edges or bitterness in it. Everyone, from the high-born to the lowly, the rich to the poor, called him by the nickname "Softy." Whenever he saw anyone sentenced to a whipping in the temple or the government office <term>yamen</term> <note>original: "yamen" — the office or residence of a Chinese or Korean government official.</note>, Namnu always begged that he might take the prisoner’s place. Once, when there was a great ceremony in progress at Pagoda Temple and many high officials were gathered, Softy was also seen kneeling at the side and participating. He suddenly remarked to Prince Hong of Yon-san, "You are truly a very great man."

Hong replied, "What do you mean by 'great man,' you impudent brat? Take that!" and he punched him in the ear with his fist. Softy laughed and said, "Please, Hong, don’t do that! It hurts! It hurts!"

Later, I was in the traveling party <note>original: "train" — a group of followers or attendants accompanying a person of high rank.</note> of Prince Yi of Yun—

...song <note>This completes the name "Prince Yi of Yunsong" from the previous page.</note>, and other high officials were present when we stopped for a short while before the temple. Softy was there, and he looked at Yi and said, “I know your face, but I have forgotten your name.” Afterwards he said, “Oh, I remember now; you are Yi Sok-hyong.” 

The monks <note>original: "priests"</note> of the monastery who heard this informal address <note>original: "familiarity"</note> were scandalized and hurried to offer endless apologies to the Prince, saying, “Softy was born this way; God <note>In this context, Gale likely translates "Heaven" or "Nature" as "God."</note> made him so. Please, Your Excellency, forgive him.” The Prince forgave him, and so he was not punished <note>original: "disturbed"</note>.

## XLIII

# THE PROPITIOUS MAGPIE
<note>original: "Propitious" means giving or indicating a good chance of success; favorable.</note>

People say that when a magpie builds its nest directly south of a home, the master of the house will be promoted in office. King T'ai-jong <note>T'ai-jong (reigned 1400–1418) was the third king of the Joseon Dynasty and a powerful leader known for stabilizing the Korean state.</note> once had a friend who was very poor and had failed in all of his endeavors. After various failed attempts to improve his situation, he decided to wait until the King went out on a royal procession. He then sent a servant to build an imitation magpie’s nest in a favorable spot right in the King's path. When the King saw it, he asked the man what he was doing. The servant replied that when a magpie builds its nest directly south of a home, the master of the house receives an immediate promotion. He explained that his master had waited so long for this to happen naturally without success, that he was now building a fake nest to bring it about. The King took pity on him and ordered his appointment to an office at once.

When I was young, a magpie built its nest in front of our home. However, I—along with some other boys—cut off the branch so that the entire nest fell to the ground. There lay the young chicks with their pitiful yellow mouths. I felt sorry for them and was afraid that they...

<summary>This page recounts a folklore belief that a magpie nesting south of a house brings a promotion, illustrated by a story of King T'ai-jong helping a poor friend who tried to "fake" this good omen.</summary>
<keywords>magpie, King T'ai-jong, promotion, superstition, Korean folklore, omens, Joseon Dynasty</keywords>

--- Page 217 ---
<meta>This page continues the personal testimony of scholar Yi Ryuk regarding the superstition that magpie nests built to the south of a residence bring professional success.</meta>

<page-num>201</page-num>

# THE PROPITIOUS MAGPIE
<note>original: "Propitious" means favorable or giving a good omen for the future.</note>

...would die. So, in a favorable location to the south, I had the nest hung up on a *zelkova* <note>original: "neutie" (neuti-namu), the Korean name for the zelkova tree, which is often regarded as a protective village tree</note> tree, where the young birds all lived, flourished, and eventually flew away. That very winter, my father was promoted three ranks and was assigned to the office of the Prime Minister.

Later, I built a summer house at Chong-pa <note>A neighborhood in modern-day Seoul</note>. In front of the house, directly facing south, magpies built a nest in a date tree. I had a female servant who pulled the nest down and used it for firewood, but the birds returned the following year and built it once again. The following year was 1469, when King Ye-jong <note>Ye-jong (reigned 1468–1469) was the eighth king of the Joseon Dynasty</note> ascended the throne. That year, I was promoted again. In the spring of 1471, magpies came and built their nest in a tree just south of my office. I laughed and said, "There is surely a spiritual power in the magpie, just as people have said since ancient times and as I have proven for myself."

->YI RYUK<-
<note>Yi Ryuk (1438–1498) was a prominent Korean scholar and official during the early Joseon period.</note>

## THE "OLD BUDDHA"

Prime Minister Choi Yun-tok <note>Choi Yun-tok (1376–1445) was a prominent general and high-ranking official during the early Joseon Dynasty, known for his military leadership and humble character.</note> was once in mourning for his mother. Traveling with only a single horse and one servant, he made a journey to the south, where the road led through Kai-ryong County. At that very time, two or three of the district magistrates had pitched a tent on the riverbank and were enjoying some refreshments. They said to one another, "Who is that mourner riding by without dismounting? <note>In traditional Korean society, it was a requirement of etiquette to dismount from one's horse when passing by officials or social superiors as a sign of respect.</note> He must be some country farmer who has never learned proper manners. We shall certainly have to teach him a lesson."

They sent an attendant to arrest the traveler's servant and bring him before them. They asked, "Who is your master?"

The servant replied, "Choi, the Old Buddha." <note>Choi Yun-tok was nicknamed "The Old Buddha" because of his gentle, upright nature and his lack of greed or pretension.</note>

"But what is his real name?" they demanded.

"The Old Buddha," was the reply.

Then they grew very angry at this and said, "Your master has offended us by not dismounting, and you offend us by concealing his name. Both servant and master are equally ill-mannered." And so, they beat the servant over the head.

<summary>Prime Minister Choi Yun-tok, traveling humbly in mourning clothes, is mistaken for a commoner by arrogant local magistrates who beat his servant for not showing proper "respect" or revealing the Prime Minister's true identity.</summary>
<keywords>Choi Yun-tok, Old Buddha, Joseon Dynasty, etiquette, mourning customs, Kai-ryong, social class, magistrates</keywords>

<header>THE "OLD BUDDHA"</header>
<page-num>203</page-num>

Then the servant said slowly, “He is called Choi the Buddha, but his real name is Yun-tok, and he is now on his way to his country home in Chang-won.” At once they recognized that it was none other than the Prime Minister, and a great fear overcame them. They quickly <term>struck their tent</term> <note>original: "struck their tent"; meaning they hurriedly took down and packed up their temporary shelter</note>, cleared away the food, and ran to make their deepest bows <note>original: "salaams"; the translator used this Middle Eastern term to describe the deep, formal prostrations (jeol) required when showing respect to a high-ranking Korean official</note> and to ask pardon for their offense.

The "Old Buddha" was a special nickname by which this famous minister was known.

->**YI RYUK**<-

### XLV

## A WONDERFUL MEDICINE

PRINCE CHEUNG had served as the First Minister <note>The highest-ranking civil official in the Joseon government, similar to a Prime Minister</note> of the country for thirty years. He was a just and upright man, now nearly ninety years old. His son was named Whal and was second in influence only to his father. Both were highly renowned in the era in which they lived, and the King treated them with special respect. 

Prince Cheung's home was suddenly plagued by goblins and demons. When a young official came to visit him, these mysterious beings snatched the hat from his head in broad daylight and crumpled it up. They threw stones as well, and kept on throwing them until the entire household was thrown into chaos. Prince Cheung escaped and moved to another house, where he prepared a special medicine called *sal-kwi-hwan* <note>original: "sal-kwi-whan"; from the Sino-Korean *sal* (kill), *kwi* (ghost/spirit), and *hwan* (pill)</note> (kill-devil pills), which he offered with a prayer. From that time on, the goblins departed, and even now, five or six years later, there has been no sign of them. Prince Cheung himself remains healthy, strong, and free from illness.

->**YI RYUK**<-

<vocab>**Prince Cheung**: A high-ranking official; "Prince" here refers to a title of nobility (Goon) often granted to merit subjects or relatives of the King. **Whal**: The son of Prince Cheung. **Sal-kwi-hwan**: A traditional medicinal or ritual preparation intended to drive away evil spirits. **Yi Ryuk**: A 15th-century scholar and author known for collecting tales of the supernatural.</vocab>

<summary>This story describes how the elderly First Minister Prince Cheung drove away a plague of mischievous goblins from his home using a special ritual medicine called "kill-devil pills."</summary>
<keywords>Prince Cheung, goblins, Korean folklore, sal-kwi-hwan, Yi Ryuk, supernatural medicine, Joseon Dynasty</keywords>

## XLVI

### FAITHFUL MO

Prince Ha <note>A title usually reserved for members of the royal family or high-ranking merit subjects</note> had a slave who was a wealthy landowner and lived in Yang-ju County. This slave had a daughter who was the fairest of the fair, whom he called Mo (meaning "Nobody" <note>original: "Mo"; in Korean, this character is often used as a placeholder for a name, similar to "a certain person" or "so-and-so"</note>). She was beautiful beyond expression. An Yun was a famous scholar and a man of great distinction in literature. He saw Mo, fell in love with her, and took her as his wife. 

When Prince Ha heard of this, he was furious. He said, "How dare you, a slave, marry a man of the aristocracy?" <note>In the Joseon Dynasty's strict caste system, children usually inherited the status of their mother. Marriage between the *yangban* (aristocracy) and *nobi* (slaves) was socially scandalous and often legally problematic.</note> He had her arrested and brought to his house, intending to marry her to one of his own bondsmen <note>Male slaves or servants bound to a master</note>. Mo received this news with tears and sorrow, and she did not know what to do. At last, she escaped over the wall and went back to An Yun. An was delighted beyond words to see her; however, considering the power of the old prince, he did not know how to protect her. Together, they swore an oath to die rather than be parted.

Later, when Prince Ha learned of this, he sent his underlings to arrest her again and carry her away. After this, all trace of her was lost, until Mo was dis— <note>The text breaks here and continues on the next page.</note>

<summary>This story introduces Mo, the beautiful daughter of a wealthy slave, who marries a scholar named An Yun; however, her father's master, Prince Ha, intervenes to stop the marriage due to their different social classes.</summary>

<keywords>Prince Ha, Mo, An Yun, Yang-ju, social class, Joseon Dynasty, slavery, romance, aristocracy</keywords>

<page-num>206</page-num>

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

—covered one day in a room, hanging by the neck, dead.

Months of sorrow passed for An until one night, under the cover of darkness, he was returning from the Confucian Temple <note>The Munmyo, a shrine dedicated to Confucius and his disciples, located in Seoul</note> to his house over the ridge of Camel Mountain <note>Naksan, a mountain on the eastern side of Seoul</note>. It was early autumn, and the wooded peaks were shimmering in the moonlight. The rest of the world had fallen softly to sleep, and no other travelers were on the road. An was thinking longingly of Mo, reciting love poems in her memory with a broken voice, when he suddenly heard a soft footfall coming from among the pine trees. He looked closely, and there stood Mo. An knew she had been dead for a long time and must have realized it was her spirit; however, because his mind was so consumed with thoughts of her, he did not doubt her presence. He ran to her and took her hand, asking, "How did you come here?" but she vanished. An cried out and burst into tears. Because of this encounter, he became ill. Although he tried to eat, his grief was so intense that he could not swallow, and shortly after, he died of a broken heart.

Kim Champan <note>original: "Champan"; a high-ranking government title, equivalent to a Vice-Minister</note>, who was my close friend and the same age as I, was also An’s cousin; he spoke of this often. Yu Hyo-jang, An's nephew by marriage, also told the story many times. He said, "She was faithful until death. For even a woman born into the literati <note>The *yangban*, or the educated scholar-official class of the Joseon Dynasty</note>..."

<summary>Following Mo’s tragic suicide, her grieving husband An Yun encounters her ghost on Camel Mountain and subsequently dies of a broken heart. The author provides historical witnesses, such as Kim Champan, to attest to the story’s truth and Mo’s extraordinary faithfulness.</summary>

<header>FAITHFUL MO</header>

...and even for those raised within the strict gates of Confucian etiquette <note>original: "ceremonial form"; referring to the complex social and moral codes of the Korean upper class</note>, it is difficult enough to choose death over dishonor. But what about her—a slave, the lowest of the low, who never received an education in the principles of Propriety, Righteousness, Truth, or Devotion? <note>These are the core Confucian virtues (the *O-sang*) expected of the nobility, but rarely expected of the enslaved.</note> Yet, until the very end, out of love for her husband, she maintained her purity and gave up her life without a single flaw. Even among the most faithful people of ancient times, was there ever anyone better than Mo?

## THE RENOWNED MAING

Minister of State Maing Sa-song <note>Maeng Sa-seong (1360–1438) was a celebrated high-ranking official of the early Joseon Dynasty, famous for his integrity and for traveling without the usual pomp of his office.</note> once upon a time, dressed in simple, ordinary clothes, started south on a long journey. On the way, he was caught in the rain and turned into a roadside pavilion for rest and shelter. There was a young scholar already in the pavilion named Whang Eui-hon, who, with his hands behind his back, was reading the pavilion's inscription board on which verses of poetry were written. He read for a long time and looked around as though no one else were there. At last, he turned to the old man and said, "Well, old-timer, do you understand the essence <note>original: "flavour"</note> of verses like these?"

The famous Minister, pretending to be ignorant, stood up and said, "How could you expect an old countryman like myself to know? Please, tell me the meaning."

Whang said, "These verses were written by the great men of the past. What they saw and experienced, they wrote down to inspire the souls of those who were to come after them. They are like pic—" <note>The sentence is interrupted by the page break and continues on the following page.</note>

...tures of sea and land, for there are living pictures in poetry, you know.”

A little later, pack-horses arrived loaded with all sorts of supplies. There were servants and retainers, too—a great company of them—carrying tent poles, canvas packs, and other equipment in a long procession.

Whang, surprised by this, made an inquiry. To his amazement, he learned that the old man was none other than Maing Sa-song <note>Maeng Sa-seong (1360–1438) was a high-ranking official of the Joseon Dynasty famous for his integrity and simple lifestyle.</note>. Instinctively, he dropped to his knees in a deep and long bow <note>original: "obeisance"</note>. 

The Minister laughed and said, “That is enough. There is no difference in the inherent value of men; they are high or low according to the thoughts that drive them. Unfortunately, however, all are born with a proud heart. You are not an ordinary scholar; why, therefore, were you so proud to begin with and so humble now?” 

The Minister took him by the hand, led him to his mat, made him sit down, comforted him, and sent him on his way.

# XLVIII

## THE SENSES

The eyes are round like gems, so that they can roll about and see things; the ears have holes in them so that they can hear; the nose has openings by which it can detect smells; and the mouth is horizontal and wide <note>original: "slit"</note> so that it can inhale and exhale the breath; the tongue is like an organ reed <term>organ reed: The vibrating part of a wind instrument, such as the traditional Korean saenghwang (mouth organ), which creates sound through breath</term> so that it can make sounds and talk. 

Three of these four organs have their own specific functions <note>original: "office"</note> to fulfill, while the mouth has two functions. However, the organ <note>original: "member"</note> that distinguishes the good from the bad is the heart. Without the heart, even though you have eyes, you cannot truly see; though you have ears, you cannot truly hear; though you have a nose, you cannot smell; and though you have a mouth, you cannot breathe. Thus, they say that without the heart, "seeing, you cannot see, and hearing, you cannot hear" <note>This reflects the Confucian and Buddhist idea that physical perception is useless without a "heart-mind" (sim) to process and provide moral direction</note>.

->YI RYUK.<-

<summary>A brief philosophical essay by Yi Ryuk describing the physical functions of the eyes, ears, nose, and mouth, concluding that the heart is the essential center of all perception and moral judgment.</summary>
<keywords>The Senses, Yi Ryuk, heart-mind, perception, philosophy, anatomy, morality</keywords>

# XLIX

## WHO DECIDES, GOD OR THE KING?

KING TAEJONG <note>Taejong (r. 1400–1418) was the third king of the Joseon Dynasty, known for strengthening royal power</note> was resting in Heunyang Palace, while outside two eunuchs were debating the law that governs human affairs—specifically, whether destiny is determined by man or by God <note>original: "God." In this Korean context, the author refers to *Haneunim* or "Heaven," the supreme governing power of the universe</note>. 

Eunuch A said, “Wealth and honor are entirely in the king’s hands.” 

Eunuch B replied, “Not at all; every bit of wealth and every level of promotion are ordained by God. Even the king himself has no part in it and no power over it.”

They continued to argue, each insisting he was right, without ever reaching an agreement.

The King, having overheard their conversation, wrote a secret dispatch saying, “Promote the bearer of this letter by one rank.” He sealed it and commanded Eunuch A to take it to Sejong <note>Sejong the Great (r. 1418–1450), Taejong's son and successor, who often managed administrative duties during his father's later years</note>, who was then in charge of that office. 

A bowed and departed, but just as he was about to leave the palace grounds, he was seized by a sharp pain in his stomach. He begged Eunuch B to take his place and go into the city.

The next day, when the record of promotions...

<header>THREE THINGS MASTERED</header>

## THREE THINGS MASTERED

There was a relative of the king named Im Sung-jong, who was a man of great intellect and ambition. He was the greatest performer of his time on the harp <note>In the context of Korean music, "harp" usually refers to the *geomungo*, a traditional six-stringed zither</note>. King Se-jong <note>Sejong the Great (r. 1418–1450), the fourth king of the Joseon Dynasty, famous for creating the Korean alphabet</note> said of him, "Im’s harp knows but one master, and it follows no other man."

His home was outside the South Gate <note>Namdaemun, the main southern gate of the old walled city of Seoul</note>. Every morning, he was seen kneeling on the doorstep of his front door, beating his hands up and down on his knees; he carried on this practice for three years. People could not imagine what he meant by it and thought he was mad. However, this was how he learned the rhythmic motions required for the harp.

Additionally, he practiced blowing with his mouth and moving his fingers day and night without stopping. When people came to visit him, he would see them with his eyes but would not truly notice <note>original: "perceive"</note> them because of his intense focus. He kept this up for three years and, in doing so, mastered the skills required for the flute <note>likely the *daegeum*, a large bamboo transverse flute</note>.

He was a man of slight build and was unskilled at horseback riding and archery. He often sighed over these shortcomings, saying, "Though I am weak and..."

<summary>This page introduces Im Sung-jong, a relative of King Se-jong, who becomes a master of the harp and flute through years of obsessive, singular practice. The story highlights the Joseon-era ideal of mastery through extreme dedication and mental focus.</summary>

<keywords>Im Sung-jong, King Se-jong, South Gate, geomungo, flute, mastery, dedication, Joseon Dynasty, Seoul</keywords>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Im Sung-jong's journey toward mastery, following his successful efforts with the harp and flute described on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>214</page-num>

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

"...unskilled <note>original: "stupid"</note> and unable to shoot over a long distance, I will still learn how to hit the target and strike the bull’s-eye. This, too, must be achieved through practice.” 

So, every morning he took his bow and arrows and went off into the hills. There he practiced shooting all day long, continuing this for three years until he became a renowned archer. From this, you can see the kind of man he was.

->YI RYUK <note>Yi Ryuk (1438–1498) was a scholar-official of the early Joseon period known for his collection of stories and essays</note><-

<header>LI</header>

## STRANGELY STRICKEN DEAD

There was once a man named Kim Tok-saing, a professional soldier <note>original: "soldier of fortune"; in this context, it refers to a career military man who rose through merit</note> who had been specially honored at the Court of King Taejong <note>Taejong (r. 1400–1418) was the third king of the Joseon Dynasty, credited with strengthening the central government</note>. He had several times served as the supreme commander <note>original: "generalissimo"</note> of the army, and on his various campaigns, he always had a close friend accompany him—a friend whom he loved dearly. 

However, Kim had been dead for more than ten years when, one night, this friend of his woke up suddenly and gave a loud cry. He fell asleep again, but a little later he was disturbed once more by a fright, at which he shouted out. His wife, troubled by this, asked what he meant. The friend said, “I have just seen General Kim riding on a white horse, with a bow and arrows at his belt. He called to me and said, ‘A thief has just entered my home, and I have come to shoot him dead.’ He went away and then returned, and as he drew an arrow from his quiver, I saw that there were bloodstains on it. He said, ‘I have just shot him; he is dead.’” The husband and wife talked over the matter together in fear and wonder.

<vocab>Kim Tok-saing, Taejong, supreme commander, professional soldier, ghost, Korea</vocab>

<summary>A former friend of the late General Kim Tok-saing experiences a vivid, terrifying vision in which the general's ghost appears on a white horse to defend his home from a thief. The ghost returns with a blood-stained arrow, claiming to have successfully killed the intruder.</summary>

<keywords>Kim Tok-saing, King Taejong, ghost story, archery, protection from beyond, Joseon Dynasty, supernatural vision</keywords>

When morning came, the friend went to General Kim's former home to investigate. He learned that on that very night, Kim's young widow had decided to remarry. However, as soon as the chosen *fiancé* <note>In the Joseon period, the remarriage of widows—especially those from the noble class—was often socially and legally discouraged, which provides the cultural context for the ghost's violent intervention</note> had entered her home, a terrible pain shot through him, and before morning came, he died in great agony.

## LII
## THE MYSTERIOUS HOI TREE <note>original: "Hoi tree"; this is the *Hoenamu*, or Chinese Scholar Tree (*Sophora japonica*), which was traditionally believed in Korea to be a dwelling place for spirits</note>

Prince Pa-song’s house was located just inside the great East Gate <note>The *Heunginjimun*, the main eastern gate in the Fortress Wall of Seoul</note>, and in front of it stood a large Hoi tree. One night, the Prince’s son-in-law was traveling along the road that led past the archers' pavilion. There he saw a large company of bowmen, more than he could count, all shooting together at a target. 

A moment later, he saw them practicing riding: some were throwing spears, some were hurling bowls <note>This may refer to a traditional military exercise or a game similar to stone-throwing or polo</note>, and others were shooting from horseback. The road in front of the pavilion was so crowded that it was blocked to all travelers. 

As he passed by, some of them shouted, “Look at that insolent rascal! He tries to ride past without dismounting!” <note>In Joseon-era Korea, it was a strict rule of etiquette to dismount from one's horse when passing people of higher rank or certain public buildings</note> They seized him and beat him, ignoring his cries for mercy and showing no pity for his pain, until a tall man stepped out from their crowded ranks and said angrily to the group, “He is my master; why are you treating him this way?” He untied the son-in-law's bonds, took him by the arm, and led him home. When the son-in-law reached the gate, he looked back and saw the man...

<summary>Prince Pa-song's son-in-law encounters a ghostly troop of soldiers practicing military arts near Seoul's East Gate. After being attacked for failing to show respect, he is rescued by a mysterious tall man who claims to be his servant.</summary>

<keywords>Prince Pa-song, East Gate, Hoi tree, Korean folklore, archery, Joseon etiquette, ghosts, Seoul</keywords>

walk under the scholar tree <note>original: "Hoi tree"; the *Hoenamu* or Chinese Scholar Tree</note> and disappear. He then learned, too, that the entire crowd of archers were spirits rather than men. He also realized that the tall man who had befriended him was a spirit as well, and that he had emerged from the specific scholar tree that stood by their house.

<header>LIII</header>

### TA-HONG

[SIM HEUI-SU studied as a young man under <note>original: "at the feet of"</note> No Su-sin, who had been sent into exile on a distant island in the sea. Sim followed his master there and worked at studying the Confucian classics <note>original: "Sacred Books"</note>. He passed the lower civil service exam <note>original: "matriculated"</note> in 1570 and passed the higher civil service exam <note>original: "graduated"</note> in 1572. In 1589, he protested <note>original: "remonstrated"</note> to King Seonjo <note>reigned 1567–1608</note> regarding the disorders of his reign and was instrumental in suppressing a major national uprising. However, he made a social blunder <note>original: "faux pas"</note> one day when he joked to a friend—

->“These sea-gull waves ride so high,<-
->Who can tame them?”<-

Those who heard this seized upon his words, and they became a source of unpopularity for him, as the poem suggested he held a negative opinion of the Royal Court.

In 1592, when the King escaped to Uiju <note>original: "Eui-ju"</note> to flee the invading Japanese army <note>This refers to the Imjin War (1592–1598)</note>, Sim served as the State’s Chief Secretary, and after the King’s return, he became the Chief Justice. He eventually resigned from his office, but the King refused to accept his resignation, saying, “I cannot do without you.” He

<summary>This page introduces the historical figure Sim Heui-su, detailing his education under an exiled master and his rise to high political office despite a controversial poem that offended the Royal Court.</summary>

<keywords>Sim Heui-su, No Su-sin, King Seonjo, Ta-hong, Uiju, Japanese invasion, Imjin War, Confucian classics, Korean history</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of the statesman Sim Heui-su, detailing his political integrity and his retirement before beginning the actual folktale.</meta>

<page-num>220</page-num>

<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

He became the leader of the scholars <note>original: "literati"</note> and a Special Adviser. Afterward, he served as the Minister of the Right and then as the Minister of the Left <note>These were the Second and First State Councillors, the highest administrative positions in the Joseon government</note>. During this time, he drafted ten recommendations for His Majesty the King to follow. He recognized the corruption surrounding the King and resigned from his office many times, but he was constantly called back to service.

In 1608, a young candidate named Im Suk-yong, while writing for his civil service examination <note>original: "matriculation"</note>, wrote an essay exposing the scandals of the Royal Court. Sim heard of this and took the young man under his protection. When the King read the essay, he was furiously angry and ordered that Im be stripped of his status <note>original: "degradation"</note>. However, Sim said, "He is with me; I stand behind what he wrote and I approve of it. Punish me and not him." Because of this, the King withdrew his anger. Sim was the most faithful of the faithful.

When he was old, he went to live in Tun-san in a small, dilapidated hut, living like the poorest of scholars. He called himself "Water-thunder Muddy-man" <note>original: "Su-roe-jin-in"</note>, a name derived from the *I Ching* <note>original: "Book of Changes," an ancient Chinese divination text</note>.

He died in 1622 at the age of seventy-four and is recorded as one of Korea's great patriots.]

### *The Story*

Minister Sim Heui-su was, in his youth, as handsome as polished marble and as white as snow, with a rare and beautiful physical form. When he was eight years old—

By that age, he was already an expert in writing Chinese characters <note>original: "adept at the character"</note> and a marvel in the eyes of his people. The boy’s nickname was Soondong (The Godlike One). From the time he passed his first examination, he rose step by step through the ranks until he at last became the First Minister of the land. In his old age, he was honored as the most renowned of all government ministers. Even at seventy, he still held office. One day, while occupied with the affairs of State, he suddenly said to those around him, "Today is my last day on earth. My farewell wish to you all is that you may prosper and act bravely and well."

His associates replied in amazement, "Your Excellency is still strong and healthy, and capable of many more years of work; why do you speak this way?"

Sim replied with a laugh, "Our lifespan is fixed. Why should I not know when mine ends? We cannot pass the predestined limit. Please, feel no regret. Use all your efforts to serve His Majesty the King, and show your gratitude for his many kindnesses <note>original: "favours"</note>."

After he had encouraged them in this way, he took his leave. Everyone wondered at this strange announcement. From that day on, he did not return to office, as it was said that he was falling ill.

At that time, there was a young secretary in the War Office who served directly under Sim. Hearing that his master was ill, the young man went to pay—

“When everything was over and the guests had gone their separate ways, I went home.
