About ten days later, I was walking back from my teacher’s house along the main street, carrying my books under my arm, when I suddenly met a beautiful girl. She was elegantly dressed and riding a fine horse. She dismounted right in front of me and, to my surprise, took my hand and said, ‘Are you not Sim Heui-su?’

“In my astonishment, I looked at her and saw that it was Ta-hong. I replied, ‘Yes, but how do you know who I am?’ I was not married then, so I did not have my hair tied up in a topknot <note>term: "sangtu"—the traditional topknot worn by married men in Korea. With diligence, I grew to be superior to all my competitors. It was five years after my farewell to Ta-hong that I won my honors <note>original: "honours"</note>. I was still only a young man, and everyone rejoiced in my success. But my true joy lay in the secret understanding that the time had finally come for me to meet Ta-hong. 

On the first day of my graduation celebrations, I expected to meet her, but I did not. The second day passed, and I saw nothing of her. Even as the third day was passing, no word had reached me. My heart was so disturbed that I found not the slightest joy in the honors of the occasion. 

Evening was falling when my father said to me, "I have a friend from my younger days who now lives in the Chang-eui district <note>original: "ward"</note>, and you must go and visit him this evening before the three days of celebration are over." "When I met Ta-hong, I was extremely happy, especially as I heard of her years of faithfulness. I simply allowed her to be called my concubine in name only, so that my nephews or other younger members of the family <note>original: "clan"</note> would not try to take her away. She is, above all, a faithful woman; I have never known anyone like her before.'

"The old man then sent the horse and the servants back, along with a letter to my parents explaining that I would be staying the night. He ordered the servants to prepare a room, decorating it with beautiful folding screens and embroidered mats, hanging up lanterns, and arranging everything as if for a bridegroom. In this way, he celebrated our reunion.

"The next morning, I said goodbye <note>original: "bade good-bye"</note> and went to tell my parents everything about my meeting with Ta-hong and what had occurred. They gave their consent that...

<vocab>Ta-hong, concubine, clan, devotion, faithfulness, His Excellency</vocab>

<summary>The elderly nobleman reveals that Ta-hong's status as his concubine was a pretense to protect her virtue, and he graciously hosts a symbolic wedding celebration for the reunited couple.</summary>
<keywords>Ta-hong, Sim Heui-su, faithfulness, virtue, marriage customs, hospitality, family clan</keywords>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Sim Heui-su and Ta-hong. After their reunion at the old nobleman's house, Ta-hong is officially brought into Sim's family home, where she proves to be an ideal and virtuous wife.</meta>

<page-num>231</page-num>
<header>TA-HONG</header>

I should have her, and so she was brought to our home and became a member of our family—really, my only true wife.

"Her life and conduct were far beyond the ordinary. In serving her superiors and helping those below her, she fulfilled every requirement of the traditional moral code <note>original: "ancient code"; referring to the Confucian ethics governing family relationships and social conduct</note>. She was faithful in her duties, and her talents in music and chess were truly exceptional. I loved her more than words can express.

"A little later, I was appointed as magistrate of Geumsan <note>original: "Keumsan"</note> County in Jeolla <note>original: "Chulla"</note> Province, and Ta-hong accompanied me. We lived there for two years. She often declined my invitations for leisure, saying that spending too much happy time together might interfere with my efficiency and official duties. One day, quite unexpectedly, she came to me and requested a private moment alone, as she had something important to tell me. I asked her what it was, and she said, 'I am going to die, for my allotted span of life is finished. Let us be happy together one last time and forget all the sorrows of the world.' I was astonished when I heard this. I could not believe it was true and asked her how she could possibly know beforehand that she was going to die. She replied, 'I simply know; there is no mistake about it.'

"Four or five days later she fell ill, though it did not seem serious; and yet, within a day or two, she passed away. As she was dying, she said to me, 'Our lives are predestined; Heaven <note>original: "God"; in this context, the translator refers to the Korean concept of *Cheon* or *Sangje*, the supreme ruler of the universe who decides human fate</note> decides everything. While I lived, I devoted myself entirely to you...'

<summary>Sim Heui-su describes Ta-hong's exemplary life as his wife and her time with him during his magistracy in Geumsan, which ends with her sudden and accurately predicted death.</summary>
<keywords>Ta-hong, Sim Heui-su, Geumsan, Jeolla Province, Confucian virtue, prophecy of death, fate</keywords>

--- Page 248 ---
<meta>This page concludes Sim Heui-su’s account of his life with Ta-hong, describing her death, his tribute to her, and her continued presence as a guardian spirit.</meta>

<page-num>232</page-num>
<header>KOREAN IMPS, GHOSTS AND FAIRIES</header>

"...and you responded most kindly in return. I have no regrets. As I die, I only ask that my body be buried where it may rest by my husband's side <note>original: "master"; Ta-hong uses the formal term of respect for her husband, common in the Joseon era</note> when he passes away, so that when we meet in the afterlife <note>original: "regions beyond"</note>, I will be with you once again." After she said this, she died.

“Her face was beautiful—not like the face of the dead, but like the face of the living. I was plunged into the deepest grief and prepared her body for burial with my own hands. Our custom is that when a secondary wife <note>original: "second wife"; refers to a concubine or wife of lower status, who traditionally would not be permitted burial in the main family plot</note> dies, she is not buried with the family, but I made an excuse and had her interred in our family burial site in Goyang <note>original: "Ko-yang"</note> County. I did this to fulfill her wishes. When I reached Geumchang <note>original: "Keum-chang"</note> during my sorrowful journey, I wrote a poem:

> ‘O beautiful Bud of the beautiful Flower,
> We carry your body on the willow bier <note>a frame or stand on which a coffin is placed; willow was often used in funeral rites</note>;
> Where has your sweet, perfumed soul gone?
> The rains fall on us
> To tell us of your tears and of your faithful heart.’

“I wrote this as a tribute of love to my faithful Ta-hong. After her death, whenever anything serious was about to happen in my home, she always came to warn me beforehand, and her predictions were never wrong. This has continued for several years, until a few days ago, when she appeared in a dream and said, ‘Master, the time of your departure has come, and we are soon to meet again.’"

<vocab>Ta-hong, Goyang, Geumchang, burial, secondary wife, dream, ghost, funeral poem</vocab>

<summary>Sim Heui-su recounts the death of his beloved wife Ta-hong, how he defied custom to bury her in his family plot, and how her spirit has continued to provide him with prophetic warnings from beyond the grave.</summary>
<keywords>Sim Heui-su, Ta-hong, funeral customs, Goyang, Geumchang, ancestral burial, guardian spirits, prophetic dreams</keywords>

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Ta-hong and the scholar Sim Heui-su. It describes Sim’s final vision of Ta-hong and his peaceful death as predicted by her spirit.</meta>

<page-num>233</page-num>
<header>TA-HONG</header>

I am now preparing to welcome you joyfully.'

"For this reason, I have said goodbye to all my colleagues. Last night she came once more and said to me, 'Tomorrow is your day.' We wept together in the dream as we met and talked. In the morning, when I woke up, the marks of tears were still on my cheeks. This is not because I am afraid to die, but because I have seen my Ta-hong. Now that you have asked me, I have told you everything. Tell this to no one." 

So Sim <note>Sim Heui-su (1548–1622) was a high-ranking state councilor during the Joseon period, famous for his literary talent and his lifelong devotion to Ta-hong</note> died on the following day, just as it had been foretold. Strange, indeed!

IM PANG.

->THE END<-

<vocab>Ta-hong, Sim, Im Pang</vocab>

<summary>Sim Heui-su recounts his final dream of Ta-hong, who prepares him for his death the following day. He dies peacefully as predicted, concluding this tale of lifelong devotion and supernatural connection.</summary>
<keywords>Sim Heui-su, Ta-hong, ghosts, Joseon Dynasty, death, prophecy, dreams, Im Pang</keywords>
